Rigidification of arithmetic 𝓓 \boldsymbol{\mathscr{D}} -modules and an overconvergent Riemann–Hilbert correspondence
Christopher Lazda

Abstract
. In this article, I define triangulated categories of constructible isocrystals on varieties over a perfect field of positive characteristic, in which Le Stum’s abelian category of constructible isocrystals sits as the heart of a natural t-structure. I then prove a Riemann–Hilbert correspondence, showing that, for objects admitting some (unspecified) Frobenius, this triangulated category is equivalent to the triangulated category of overholonomic 𝒟 † \mathscr{D}^{\dagger} -modules in the sense of Caro. I also show that the cohomological functors f ! , f + f^{!},f_{+} and ⊗ \otimes defined for 𝒟 † \mathscr{D}^{\dagger} -modules have natural interpretations on the constructible side of this correspondence. Finally, I use this to prove that, for any variety X X admitting an immersion into a smooth and proper formal scheme, rigid cohomology (with lisse coefficients) agrees with cohomology defined using arithmetic 𝒟 \mathscr{D} -modules.



Introduction

Let
X/{\mathbb{C}}
be a smooth algebraic variety, and
\mathscr{D}_{X}
the ring of differential operators on
X
. One of the (many!) forms of the Riemann–Hilbert correspondence states that there is an equivalence of categories

{\bf D}^{b}_{c}(X^{\mathrm{an}},{\mathbb{C}})\leftrightarrows{\bf D}^{b}_{\rm rh}(\mathscr{D}_{X})

between the bounded derived category of (algebraically) constructible sheaves of
{\mathbb{C}}
-modules on
X^{\mathrm{an}}
, and the bounded derived category of regular holonomic
\mathscr{D}_{X}
-modules. Moreover, this equivalence matches up various natural cohomological operations on each side, namely the ‘six functors’ of usual and extraordinary pushforward and pullback, duality, and tensor product.

From a differential geometer’s perspective, this gives a way of understanding differential equations on
X
by studying their associated constructible sheaves. But from an algebro-geometric point of view, it shows that the cohomology theory of constructible sheaves on
X^{\mathrm{an}}
can be reconstructed completely algebraically, via the theory of regular holonomic
\mathscr{D}
-modules on
X
. It therefore provides a template for understanding the cohomology of algebraic varieties in situations where taking the complex analytification is not possible, for example when working in characteristic
p
.

This idea has been the one of the main driving forces behind two different approaches to studying the
p
-adic cohomology of varieties in characteristic
p
, both developed by Berthelot. The first of these to be introduced was the theory of rigid cohomology
[
Ber96a
]
, generalising earlier work of Monsky–Washnitzer
[
MW68
]
. Rigid cohomology works as a version of de Rham cohomology on
p
-adic analytic varieties, and its coefficient objects, called overconvergent
F
-isocrystals, are therefore direct analogues of vector bundles with integrable connection. Such ‘lisse’ coefficient objects cannot support a good formalism of cohomological operations, and so Berthelot introduced in
[
Ber02
]
a theory of arithmetic
\mathscr{D}
-modules on mixed characteristic formal schemes. This is analogous to the theory of regular holonomic
\mathscr{D}
-modules on complex varieties, and was intended to play the same role in rigid cohomology as that played by the theory of constructible sheaves in
\ell
-adic étale cohomology. At least for objects admitting a Frobenius structure, it was proved by Caro–Tsuzuki in
[
CT12
]
that the category of overholonomic
\mathscr{D}^{\dagger}
-modules does indeed support a formalism of Grothendieck’s six operations.

Despite the fact that both approaches were inspired by the classical Riemann–Hilbert correspondence, they have a rather different flavour. The theory of rigid cohomology is based upon the de Rham cohomology of
p
-adic analytic varieties in characteristic
0
, whereas arithmetic
\mathscr{D}
-modules live on
p
-adic formal schemes of mixed characteristic
(0,p)
. This may seem like a fairly minor distinction, but it does significantly complicate comparisons between the two. For example, even though the theory of arithmetic
\mathscr{D}
-modules was explicitly introduced in order to provide rigid cohomology with a ‘six operations’ formalism, it has still remained open in general whether or not the rigid cohomology groups of a variety, with coefficients in an overconvergent
F
-isocrystal, coincide with the analogous cohomology groups computed using the theory of arithmetic
\mathscr{D}
-modules.

The two theories also have somewhat complementary strengths. It is rigid cohomology (and it’s earlier version, Monsky–Washnitzer cohomology) that is generally more computable, and actually appears in algorithms
[
Lau06
,
ABC+19
]
used to calculate zeta functions of algebraic varieties. On the other hand, it is the theory of arithmetic
\mathscr{D}
-modules that has a good cohomological formalism. It is therefore an important problem to relate these two approaches, and combine the strengths of both into one unified overconvergent cohomology theory.

A major step in this direction was taken by Le Stum, who in
[
LS16
]
defined a category of ‘constructible isocrystals’ on a variety in characteristic
p
. These are
direct
generalisations of overconvergent
F
-isocrystals, and like them, live on
p
-adic analytic varieties in characteristic
0
. The definition is also very closely analogous to that of the category of constructible
\ell
-adic étale sheaves. He then conjectured that there should be a Deligne–Kashiwara correspondence between his category of constructible isocrystals and a certain category of ‘perverse arithmetic
\mathscr{D}
-modules’, and proved this conjecture in the case of smooth and proper curves
[
LS14
]
, at least for objects which are of ‘Frobenius type’. One of the main results in this article proves Le Stum’s conjecture for arbitrary smooth formal schemes, albeit with a slightly stronger ‘Frobenius type’ hypothesis.

In fact, my approach will be to first establish a Riemann–Hilbert type correspondence on the level of derived categories, and then compare t-structures on both sides to deduce an equivalence between the respective hearts. The first goal of this article is therefore to define a derived analogue of Le Stum’s category of constructible isocrystals. To explain the construction, let
\mathcal{V}
be a complete DVR with fraction field
K
of characteristic
0
and perfect residue field
k
of characteristic
p>0
. The definition is the most naïve one: if
\mathfrak{P}
is a smooth formal scheme over
\mathcal{V}
, with generic fibre
\mathfrak{P}_{K}
, then a constructible complex on
\mathfrak{P}
is a bounded complex of modules over the ring
\mathscr{D}_{\mathfrak{P}_{K}}
of (algebraic) differential operators on
\mathfrak{P}_{K}
, whose cohomology sheaves are constructible isocrystals in the sense of Le Stum. The category
{\bf D}^{b}_{\mathrm{cons}}(\mathfrak{P})
of these objects is then viewed simply as a full subcategory of
{\bf D}(\mathscr{D}_{\mathfrak{P}_{K}})
. The hard work then consists of showing that this gives rise to a reasonable theory in characteristic
p
. Concretely, this amounts to showing that if
X\hookrightarrow\mathfrak{P}
is a locally closed immersion, and
\mathfrak{P}
is proper, then the full subcategory of
{\bf D}^{b}_{\mathrm{cons}}(\mathfrak{P})
consisting of objects supported on
\left]X\right[_{\mathfrak{P}}
is independent of
\mathfrak{P}
.
1
1

1

Since I will be working with adic spaces throughout, the tube
\left]X\right[_{\mathfrak{P}}
considered here behaves very much like the tubes considered in Berkovich geometry, and in particular the formalism of overconvergent sections is in some sense absorbed into the topology of
\left]X\right[_{\mathfrak{P}}
.
More generally, I show that this is the case provided that
\mathfrak{P}
is smooth in a neighbourhood of
X
, and the closure of
X
in
P
is proper over
k
. This gives rise to a derived category
{\bf D}^{b}_{\mathrm{cons}}(X)
of constructible isocrystals on
X
, which is therefore a reasonable candidate for admitting a six operations formalism (at least for objects which are of Frobenius type).

The main goal of this article is then to prove an ‘overconvergent Riemann–Hilbert correspondence’, showing that
{\bf D}^{b}_{\mathrm{cons}}(X)
is equivalent to the derived category
{\bf D}^{b}_{\mathrm{hol}}(X)
of overholonomic
\mathscr{D}^{\dagger}
-modules on
X
in the sense of Caro and Abe–Caro
[
AC18
]
(again, this will only hold for objects of Frobenius type). In fact, since I will be working with constructible objects it suffices to do this on the level of the smooth formal scheme
\mathfrak{P}
in which
X
has been embedded.

In the classical Riemann–Hilbert correspondence, the functor from
\mathscr{D}
-modules to constructible sheaves simply takes the (shifted) de Rham complex of an algebraic
\mathscr{D}
-module, but in the
p
-adic world even constructing the appropriate functor takes a little bit of work. In our previous article
[
AL22
]
we constructed a functor
\mathrm{sp}_{!}

from
constructible isocrystals
to
overholonomic
\mathscr{D}^{\dagger}
-modules. This was a rather complicated version of pushforward along
\mathrm{sp}\colon\mathfrak{P}_{K}\rightarrow\mathfrak{P}
, and only worked on the level of abelian categories. Here, I construct a functor
\mathrm{sp}^{!}
going in the other direction, which is a kind of completed pullback along
\mathrm{sp}
, shifted by the dimension of
\mathfrak{P}
(the reason for this shift will be discussed below). Conceptually, the functor
\mathrm{sp}^{!}
is much simpler than
\mathrm{sp}_{!}
, and works perfectly well on the level of derived categories. The main result of this article is then the following.

Theorem

(
8.0.1
)
.

Let
\mathfrak{P}
be a smooth formal scheme over
\mathcal{V}
,
{\bf D}^{b}_{\mathrm{cons},F}(\mathfrak{P})\subset{\bf D}^{b}_{\mathrm{cons}}(\mathfrak{P})
the full subcategory of objects of Frobenius type,
2
2

2

For the purposes of this article, I will take ‘of Frobenius type’ to mean objects which are iterated extensions of those admitting some unspecified
p^{n}
-power Frobenius structure (where
n
is allowed to vary). This coincides with the terminology from
[
AL22
]
, though not with that from
[
LS14
]
. It is what has previously been called ‘
F
-able’ in the literature.
and
{\bf D}^{b}_{\mathrm{hol},F}(\mathfrak{P})\subset{\bf D}^{b}_{\mathrm{coh}}(\mathscr{D}^{\dagger}_{\mathfrak{P}})
the full subcategory of overholonomic complexes of Frobenius type. Then
\mathrm{sp}^{!}
induces an equivalence of categories

\mathrm{sp}^{!}\colon{\bf D}^{b}_{\mathrm{hol},F}(\mathfrak{P})\overset{\cong}{\longrightarrow}{\bf D}^{b}_{\mathrm{cons},F}(\mathfrak{P}).

The very similar notation
{\bf D}^{b}_{\mathrm{hol},F}(\mathfrak{P})
and
{\bf D}^{b}_{\mathrm{cons},F}(\mathfrak{P})
hides the fact that objects in the two categories are of a very different nature. On the one hand, we have overholonomic complexes of
\mathscr{D}^{\dagger}
-modules on
\mathfrak{P}
, on the other we have complexes of modules with integrable connection on
\mathfrak{P}_{K}
whose cohomology sheaves are constructible.

As in the case of varieties over
{\mathbb{C}}
, it is important not just to have such an equivalence, but to know how it behaves with respect to the natural cohomological operations on each side. Out of the six functors
f_{+},f^{+},f_{!},f^{!},\otimes
and
{\bf D}
defined for overholonomic
\mathscr{D}^{\dagger}
-modules, I give natural interpretations of
f^{!},f_{+}
and
\otimes
as functors on the derived category of constructible complexes
{\bf D}^{b}_{\mathrm{cons}}(X)
. The extraordinary pullback
f^{!}
and tensor product
\otimes
are easy: after taking an embedding
X\hookrightarrow\mathfrak{P}
they just correspond to ordinary pullback of
\mathscr{D}_{\left]X\right[_{\mathfrak{P}}}
-modules, and ordinary tensor product over
\mathcal{O}_{\left]X\right[_{\mathfrak{P}}}
. For a smooth and proper morphism
u\colon\mathfrak{P}\rightarrow\mathfrak{Q}
of formal schemes, the functor
u_{+}
for overholonomic
\mathscr{D}^{\dagger}_{\mathfrak{P}{\mathbb{Q}}}
-modules corresponds (up to a shift) with the higher de  Rham pushfoward of constructible isocrystals. This has the following important consequence.

Corollary

(
8.0.3
)
.

Let
u\colon\mathfrak{P}\rightarrow\mathfrak{Q}
be a smooth and proper morphism of smooth formal schemes. Then the functor
{\bf R}u_{\mathrm{dR}*}
maps
{\bf D}^{b}_{\mathrm{cons},F}(\mathfrak{P})
into
{\bf D}^{b}_{\mathrm{cons},F}(\mathfrak{Q})
.

For varieties
X/k
embedded inside formal schemes, the correct interpretation of
f_{+}
on the ‘constructible’ side turns out to use the compactly supported de Rham pushforwards introduced in
[
AL20
]
(see §
9.2
for details). Given the usual duality relations amongst the six functors, the only thing missing from a complete six functor formalism on the constructible side of the correspondence is a suitable interpretation of the duality functor, and I don’t currently have a good candidate for this.

One can also also ask about the effect of
\mathrm{sp}^{!}
on t-structures. The category
{\bf D}^{b}_{\mathrm{cons},F}(\mathfrak{P})
admits a natural t-structure coming from the inclusion
{\bf D}^{b}_{\mathrm{cons},F}(\mathfrak{P})\rightarrow{\bf D}(\mathscr{D}_{\mathfrak{P}_{K}})
. On the other hand, there are three distinct t-structures on
{\bf D}^{b}_{\mathrm{hol},F}(\mathfrak{P})
that have been previously studied in the literature. The first is just the obvious one coming from the inclusion
{\bf D}^{b}_{\mathrm{hol},F}(\mathfrak{P})\subset{\bf D}^{b}_{\mathrm{coh}}(\mathscr{D}^{\dagger}_{\mathfrak{P}})
. The second is what is called the ‘constructible’ t-structure. Namely, it is the analogue of the t-structure on
{\bf D}^{b}_{\rm rh}(\mathscr{D}_{X})
given by transporting the ordinary t-structure on
{\bf D}^{b}_{c}(X^{\mathrm{an}},{\mathbb{C}})
along the classical Riemann–Hilbert correspondence. It is characterised by the fact that
f^{+}
is t-exact. The third is what I call the ‘dual constructible’ t-structure, and is defined as the Verdier dual of the constructible t-structure. It is therefore characterised by the fact that
f^{!}
is t-exact. It turns out that
\mathrm{sp}^{!}
matches up the ordinary t-structure on
{\bf D}^{b}_{\mathrm{cons},F}(\mathfrak{P})
with the dual constructible t-structure on
{\bf D}^{b}_{\mathrm{hol},F}(\mathfrak{P})
.
3
3

3

This is the reason for the shift in the definition of
\mathrm{sp}^{!}
.
Denoting the heart of the dual constructible t-structure by
{\bf DCon}_{F}(\mathfrak{P})\subset{\bf D}^{b}_{\mathrm{hol},F}(\mathfrak{P})
, and the abelian category of constructible isocrystals on
\mathfrak{P}
(of Frobenius type) by
\mathbf{Isoc}_{\mathrm{cons},F}(\mathfrak{P})
, I therefore deduce the following version of Le Stum’s conjecture.

Corollary

(
9.2.4
)
.

Let
\mathfrak{P}
be a smooth formal scheme over
\mathcal{V}
. Then
\mathrm{sp}^{!}
induces an equivalence of categories

{\bf DCon}_{F}(\mathfrak{P})\overset{\cong}{\longrightarrow}\mathbf{Isoc}_{\mathrm{cons},F}(\mathfrak{P}).

This immediately implies a similar result for varieties over
k
.

Finally, I use the equivalence
\mathrm{sp}^{!}
to prove that rigid cohomology (with coefficients) coincides with its
\mathscr{D}^{\dagger}
-module counterpart. Recall that in
[
Abe19
, §3]
, Abe defines a functor
\rho_{X}\colon\mathbf{Isoc}_{F}^{\dagger}(X)\rightarrow{\bf D}^{b}_{\mathrm{hol},F}(X)
from the category of overconvergent isocrystals of Frobenius type on a variety
X
, to the category of overholonomic complexes on
X
, building on previous work of Caro.

Corollary

(
10.0.2
)
.

Let
X
be a strongly realisable variety,
f\colon X\rightarrow\mathrm{Spec}\left(k\right)
the structure morphism, and
\mathscr{F}
an overconvergent isocrystal on
X
of Frobenius type. Then
\mathrm{sp}^{!}
induces an isomorphism

f_{+}\rho_{X}(\mathscr{F})\overset{\cong}{\longrightarrow}{\bf R}\Gamma_{\mathrm{rig}}(X,\mathscr{F})

in
{\bf D}^{b}(K)
.

Let me now give an outline of contents the article. In §
1
I recall various preliminary results that I need in the theory of analytic geometry and rigid cohomology. In particular, I state the main results that I will need on compactly supported cohomology of analytic varieties from
[
AL20
]
. In §
2
I carefully define the (abelian) category of constructible isocrystals on varieties and pairs, following Le Stum. Then in §
3
I define the analogous triangulated category, and prove that it satisfies all the expected invariance and functoriality properties. In §
4
I recall how the theory of overholonomic
\mathscr{D}^{\dagger}
-modules on varieties and pairs works, and define the three natural t-structures that exist on the derived categories of these objects. In §
5
I construct the ‘rigidification’ functor
{\bf L}\hat{\mathrm{sp}}^{*}
on the level of
\mathcal{O}
-modules, which is a kind of completed pullback along the specialisation morphism
\mathrm{sp}\colon\mathfrak{P}_{K}\rightarrow\mathfrak{P}
associated to a flat formal scheme
\mathfrak{P}
over
\mathcal{V}
. Then in §
6
I upgrade this functor to include
\mathscr{D}
-module structures, giving rise to the functor
\mathrm{sp}^{!}
from overholonomic complexes of
\mathscr{D}^{\dagger}
-modules to (complexes of) constructible isocrystals. I prove that this functor is t-exact for the dual constructible t-structure on the source and the natural t-structure on the target. In §
7
I pave the way for proving the Riemann–Hilbert correspondence by establishing a key vanishing result in log rigid cohomology, and then in §
8
I prove my main result, that
\mathrm{sp}^{!}
is an equivalence on objects of Frobenius type. Then in §
9
I describe the compatibility of
\mathrm{sp}^{!}
on the various cohomological operations defined for constructible isocrystals and overholonomic
\mathscr{D}^{\dagger}
-modules, and finally in §
10
I prove the comparison theorem between rigid and
\mathscr{D}^{\dagger}
-module cohomology.

Notation and conventions

•

I will denote by
K
a complete, discretely valued field of characteristic
0
, whose residue field
k
is perfect of characteristic
p
. While the general formalism of rigid cohomology does not require
K
to be discretely valued, or
k
to be perfect, the theory of arithmetic
\mathscr{D}^{\dagger}
-modules is generally developed under these assumptions.
4
4

4

Although the requirement that
k
is perfect has recently been lifted by
[
Car19
]
.
Since my main point of interest is comparisons between the two, I will impose this hypothesis from the beginning. The absolute value on
K
(or any valued extension thereof) will be normalised so that
\left|p\right|=p^{-1}
.

•

I will write
\mathcal{V}
for the ring of integers of
K
,
\mathfrak{m}
for its maximal ideal, and
\varpi
for a choice of uniformiser. I will fix a power
q=p^{a}
of
p
, and assume that
K
admits a lift
\sigma
of the
q
Frobenius on
k
. Frobenius will always mean the
q
-power Frobenius.

•

A
variety
will mean a separated and finite type
k
-scheme, a
formal scheme
will mean a separated and (topologically) finite type formal scheme over
\mathrm{Spf}\left(\mathcal{V}\right)
, and an
analytic variety
will mean an adic space, separated and locally of finite type over
\mathrm{Spa}\left(K,\mathcal{V}\right)
. Given a formal scheme
\mathfrak{P}
, its generic fibre
\mathfrak{P}_{K}
is therefore an analytic variety, and its special fibre
\mathfrak{P}_{k}
is a variety. I will use fraktur letters to denote formal schemes, and the corresponding roman letters for their special fibres, for example
P=\mathfrak{P}_{k}
.

•

Adjectives such as flat or smooth, when applied to varieties or formal schemes, should be understood to apply to the structure morphism to
\mathrm{Spec}\left(k\right)
or
\mathrm{Spf}\left(\mathcal{V}\right)
. Thus a smooth variety will be a variety that is smooth over
k
, and a flat formal scheme will be a formal scheme that is flat over
\mathcal{V}
. Similar adjectives applied to analytic varieties should be understood to apply to the structure morphism to
\mathrm{Spa}\left(K,\mathcal{V}\right)
.

•

If
\rho\in\sqrt{\left|K\right|}
, I will denote by
{\mathbb{D}}^{d}_{K}(0;\rho)
the closed polydisc of radius
\rho
over
K
,
5
5

5

via the normalisation
\left|p\right|=p^{-1}
and by
{\mathbb{D}}^{d}_{K}(0;\rho^{-})
the open polydisc of radius
\rho
. If
\mathscr{X}
is an analytic variety, and
\#\in\{\emptyset,-\}
, I will denote by
{\mathbb{D}}^{d}_{\mathscr{X}}(0;\rho^{\#})
the fibre product
{\mathbb{D}}^{d}_{K}(0;\rho^{\#})\times_{\mathrm{Spa}\left(K,\mathcal{V}\right)}\mathscr{X}
.

•

If
X
is a topological space, I will denote by
\mathbf{Sh}(X)
the category of (abelian) sheaves on
X
. If
\mathcal{O}_{X}
is a sheaf of (not necessarily commutative) rings on
X
, I will denote the category of coherent
\mathcal{O}_{X}
-modules by
\mathbf{Coh}(\mathcal{O}_{X})
.
6
6

6

Recall that an
\mathcal{O}_{X}
-module
\mathscr{F}
is called
coherent
if it is locally finitely generated, and the kernel of any map
\mathcal{O}_{U}^{\oplus r}\rightarrow\mathscr{F}\mid_{U}
on any open subset
U\subset X
is also locally finitely generated.

•

If
\mathcal{A}
is an abelian category I will denote by
{\bf D}^{\#}(\mathcal{A})
the derived category with boundedness condition
\#\in\{\emptyset,+,-,b\}
. If
\mathcal{A}=\mathbf{Sh}(X)
for some topological space
X
, I will usually write
\mathbf{Ch}^{\#}(X)
and
{\bf D}^{\#}(X)
instead, and if
\mathcal{A}
is the category of
\mathcal{O}_{X}
-modules on a ringed space
(X,\mathcal{O}_{X})
, I will write
\mathbf{Ch}^{\#}(\mathcal{O}_{X})
and
{\bf D}^{\#}(\mathcal{O}_{X})
.

•

If
A
is an abelian group (or more generally, a sheaf of abelian groups on a topological space), I will write
A_{{\mathbb{Q}}}
for
A\otimes_{{\mathbb{Z}}}{\mathbb{Q}}
. If
\mathcal{C}
is an additive category, I will denote by
\mathcal{C}_{{\mathbb{Q}}}
the corresponding isogeny category. Thus the objects of
\mathcal{C}_{\mathbb{Q}}
are the same as
\mathcal{C}
, but the hom sets have been tensored with
{\mathbb{Q}}
:
{\rm Hom}_{\mathcal{C}_{\mathbb{Q}}}(X,Y)={\rm Hom}_{\mathcal{C}}(X,Y)\otimes_{{\mathbb{Z}}}{\mathbb{Q}}
.

•

For a morphism
f\colon(X,\mathcal{O}_{X})\rightarrow(Y,\mathcal{O}_{Y})
of ringed spaces (or more generally, ringed sites) I will use the formalism of
K
-injective and
K
-flat resolutions from
[
Spa88
]
to define the functors
{\bf R}f_{*}
and
{\bf L}f^{*}
on unbounded derived categories of
\mathcal{O}_{X}
and
\mathcal{O}_{Y}
-modules. In order for this construction to be well-behaved, the sites
X
and
Y
under consideration will need to have bases for their topologies which are of finite cohomological dimension, this will be the case for all sites considered in this article. In particular, if
Y
is a point, then this gives the derived global sections functor
{\bf R}\Gamma(X,-)
for unbounded complexes. The internal hom functor

{\bf R}\underline{\rm Hom}_{\mathcal{O}_{X}}(-,-)\colon{\bf D}(\mathcal{O}_{X})^{\rm op}\times{\bf D}(\mathcal{O}_{X})\rightarrow{\bf D}(\mathcal{O}_{X})

can be defined similarly. By then applying
{\bf R}\Gamma(X,-)
, so can the functor
{\bf R}{\rm Hom}_{\mathcal{O}_{X}}(-,-)
taking values in
{\bf D}(\Gamma(X,\mathcal{O}_{X}))
.

•

I will use the notions of partition and stratification as defined in
[
Stacks
,
Tag 09XY
]
, although with slightly different terminology. Thus a partition of Noetherian topological space
X
is a finite decomposition
X=\bigsqcup_{\alpha\in A}X_{\alpha}
into locally closed subsets
X_{\alpha}
. It is called a stratification if
X_{\alpha}\cap\overline{X}_{\beta}\neq\emptyset\implies X_{\alpha}\subset\overline{X}_{\beta}
. This is in fact called a
good
stratification in
[
Stacks
,
Tag 09XY
]
. Every partition can be refined to a stratification.

1.
Preliminaries

In this section I recall some general results and constructions I will need, mostly in rigid analytic geometry and rigid cohomology.

1.1.
Adic spaces

In this article, analytic varieties will always be considered as adic spaces. I will therefore write
\mathbf{An}_{K}
for the category of adic spaces separated and locally of finite type over
\mathrm{Spa}\left(K,\mathcal{V}\right)
, and refer to such objects as
analytic varieties
(over
K
). By
[
Hub96
, §1.1.11]
there is an equivalence of categories

\displaystyle(-)_{0}\colon\mathbf{An}_{K}

\displaystyle\rightarrow\mathbf{Rig}_{K}

\displaystyle\mathscr{X}

\displaystyle\mapsto\mathscr{X}_{0}

between
\mathbf{An}_{K}
and the category of separated rigid analytic spaces over
K
in the sense of Tate
[
Tat71
]
. Denote a quasi-inverse to this functor by
r(-)
. If
\mathscr{X}_{\mathrm{an}}
denotes the analytic site of
\mathscr{X}
(that is, the category of open subsets of
\mathscr{X}
equipped with its canonical topology), and
\mathscr{X}_{0,G}
the
G
-site of
\mathscr{X}_{0}
(that is, the category of admissible opens equipped with the topology of admissible open coverings), then the functor

\displaystyle\mathscr{X}_{\mathrm{an}}

\displaystyle\leftarrow\mathscr{X}_{0,G}

\displaystyle r(U)

\displaystyle\mapsfrom U

induces an equivalence of toposes

\displaystyle\mathbf{Sh}(\mathscr{X})

\displaystyle\overset{\cong}{\longrightarrow}\mathbf{Sh}_{G}(\mathscr{X}_{0})

\displaystyle\mathscr{F}

\displaystyle\mapsto\mathscr{F}_{0},

which is natural in
\mathscr{X}

[
Hub96
, §1.1.11]
. In particular, it induces isomorphisms in cohomology

{\rm H}^{q}(\mathscr{X},\mathscr{F})\overset{\cong}{\longrightarrow}{\rm H}^{q}(\mathscr{X}_{0},\mathscr{F}_{0})

for any abelian sheaf
\mathscr{F}
. Since
\left(\mathcal{O}_{\mathscr{X}}\right)_{0}\overset{\cong}{\longrightarrow}\mathcal{O}_{\mathscr{X}_{0}}
, it also induces an equivalence of categories

\mathbf{Coh}(\mathcal{O}_{\mathscr{X}})\overset{\cong}{\longrightarrow}\mathbf{Coh}(\mathcal{O}_{\mathscr{X}_{0}})

between coherent sheaves on
\mathscr{X}
and
\mathscr{X}_{0}
.

1.2.
Frames and tubes

The basic objects of rigid cohomology are frames and tubes. Since the theory is generally phrased in the language of rigid analytic spaces, I will briefly discuss here the changes that need to be made when using adic spaces instead.

1.2.1 Definition
.

(1)

A pair
(X,Y)
consists of an open immersion
X\hookrightarrow Y
of varieties.
7
7

7

Implicitly, these are always
k
-varieties, and hence a pair might be more precisely termed a ‘pair over
k
’.

(2)

A frame
(X,Y,\mathfrak{P})
consists of a pair
(X,Y)
together with a closed immersion
Y\hookrightarrow\mathfrak{P}
of formal schemes, such that
\mathfrak{P}
is flat.
8
8

8

Again, this flatness is over
\mathcal{V}
, and a more precise term for a frame would be a ‘frame over
\mathcal{V}
’.

There is an obvious notion of a morphism of pairs or of frames.

The two formalisms of rigid cohomology and arithmetic
\mathscr{D}
-modules for pairs and varieties work under slightly different hypotheses on the frames involved.

1.2.2 Definition
.

Let
(X,Y,\mathfrak{P})
be a frame.

(1)

\mathfrak{P}
is smooth around
X
if there exists an open subscheme
\mathfrak{U}\subset\mathfrak{P}
, containing
X
, which is smooth over
\mathcal{V}
.

(2)

(X,Y,\mathfrak{P})
is an
l.p. frame
if
\mathfrak{P}
is smooth, and admits a locally closed immersion into a smooth and proper formal
\mathcal{V}
-scheme.

This leads to two different notions of ‘realisability’ for pairs or varieties.

1.2.3 Definition
.

(1)

A pair
(X,Y)
is said to be weakly realisable if there exists a frame
(X,Y,\mathfrak{P})
with
\mathfrak{P}
smooth around
X
.

(2)

A pair
(X,Y)
is said to be strongly realisable if there exists an l.p. frame
(X,Y,\mathfrak{P})
.

(3)

A variety
X
is said to be weakly realisable if there exists a frame
(X,Y,\mathfrak{P})
with
Y
proper and
\mathfrak{P}
smooth around
X
.

(4)

A variety
X
is said to be strongly realisable if there exists an l.p. frame
(X,Y,\mathfrak{P})
with
Y
proper.
9
9

9

Equivalently,
X
admits a locally closed immersion into a smooth and proper formal scheme.

If
\mathfrak{P}
is a formal scheme, there is a continuous specialisation map

\mathrm{sp}=\mathrm{sp}_{\mathfrak{P}}\colon\colon\mathfrak{P}_{K}\rightarrow\mathfrak{P}\cong P.

Write
[\mathfrak{P}_{K}]
for the separated quotient of
\mathfrak{P}_{K}
in the sense of
[
FK18
, Chapter 0, §2.3]
, this is the set of maximal points of
\mathfrak{P}_{K}
equipped with the quotient topology via the map

\mathrm{sep}=\mathrm{sep}_{\mathfrak{P}_{K}}\colon\mathfrak{P}_{K}\rightarrow[\mathfrak{P}_{K}]

taking a point
x
to its maximal generalisation
[x]
. Define a (non-continuous!) map

[\mathrm{sp}]=[\mathrm{sp}_{\mathfrak{P}}]\colon\mathfrak{P}_{K}\rightarrow P

as the composite

\mathfrak{P}_{K}\overset{\mathrm{sep}}{\longrightarrow}[\mathfrak{P}_{K}]\overset{\mathrm{sp}}{\longrightarrow}P

where the second map is the restriction of
\mathrm{sp}
to the subset
[\mathfrak{P}_{K}]\subset\mathfrak{P}_{K}
. The map
[\mathrm{sp}]
is in fact anti-continuous: the preimage of a closed subset is open and vice versa.

Recall that a subset
S\subset T
of a topological space
T
is called
constructible
if it lies in the Boolean algebra generated by the open subsets
U
of
T
for which the inclusion
U\rightarrow T
is a quasi-compact morphisms. In particular, if
T=P
is a variety over
k
, then the quasi-compactness condition is automatically satisfied.

1.2.4 Definition
.

For any constructible subset
S\subset P
, define the tube
\left]S\right[_{\mathfrak{P}}:=[\mathrm{sp}]^{-1}(S)\subset\mathfrak{P}_{K}
.

If
S
is closed, then
\left]S\right[_{\mathfrak{P}}
is an open subspace of
\mathfrak{P}_{K}
, and if
S
is open, then
\left]S\right[_{\mathfrak{P}}
is a closed subset of
\mathfrak{P}_{K}
. Since an implicit assumption on
\mathfrak{P}
is that it is quasi-compact, it follows that
\left]S\right[_{\mathfrak{P}}
will be a finite union of locally closed subsets of
\mathfrak{P}_{K}
. However, it will not be constructible. For example, if
\mathfrak{P}=\widehat{{\mathbb{A}}}^{1}_{\mathcal{V}}
and
S=\{0\}\subset{\mathbb{A}}^{1}_{k}
, then
\left]0\right[_{\widehat{{\mathbb{A}}}^{1}_{\mathcal{V}}}\subset{\mathbb{D}}^{1}_{K}(0;1)
is a non-quasi-compact open, and is therefore not constructible.

In general,
\left]S\right[_{\mathfrak{P}}
won’t admit any natural structure as an adic space, unless
S
is a closed subset of
P
. I can, however, always consider it as a locally ringed space by equipping it with the restriction

\mathcal{O}_{\left]S\right[_{\mathfrak{P}}}:=\mathcal{O}_{\mathfrak{P}_{K}}\!\!\mid_{\left]S\right[_{\mathfrak{P}}}

of the structure sheaf on
\mathfrak{P}_{K}
. If
i\colon S\hookrightarrow S^{\prime}
is an inclusion of constructible subsets of
P
, I will generally abuse notation and also write
i\colon\left]S\right[_{\mathfrak{P}}\rightarrow\left]S^{\prime}\right[_{\mathfrak{P}}
for the induced morphism of tubes. This is then naturally a morphism of locally ringed spaces, and if
i\colon S\hookrightarrow S^{\prime}
is a locally closed immersion, then
i\colon\left]S\right[_{\mathfrak{P}}\rightarrow\left]S^{\prime}\right[_{\mathfrak{P}^{\prime}}
is topologically the inclusion of a locally closed subset. In this case the functor

i_{!}\colon\mathbf{Sh}(\left]S\right[_{\mathfrak{P}})\rightarrow\mathbf{Sh}(\left]S^{\prime}\right[_{\mathfrak{P}})

of extension by zero along
i
is defined purely topologically. A sheaf on
\left]S^{\prime}\right[_{\mathfrak{P}}
is said to be
supported on
\left]S\right[_{\mathfrak{P}}
iff it is in the essential image of this functor (note that we are not necessarily insisting on
\left]S\right[_{\mathfrak{P}}
being closed in
\left]S^{\prime}\right[_{\mathfrak{P}}
when we say that a sheaf is supported on
\left]S\right[_{\mathfrak{P}}
).

Now, suppose that

\textstyle{X^{\prime}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{f}
\textstyle{Y^{\prime}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{g}
\textstyle{\mathfrak{P}^{\prime}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{u}
\textstyle{X\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{Y\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{\mathfrak{P}}

is a morphism of frames. Then there is an induced morphism

\left]f\right[_{u}\colon\left]X^{\prime}\right[_{\mathfrak{P}^{\prime}}\rightarrow\left]X\right[_{\mathfrak{P}}

of locally ringed spaces, with associated module pullback functor
\left]f\right[_{u}^{*}
.

1.2.5 Remark
.

When
u=\mathrm{id}
and
f
is a locally closed immersion, this will often be denoted
f\colon\left]X^{\prime}\right[_{\mathfrak{P}}\rightarrow\left]X\right[_{\mathfrak{P}}
instead of
\left]f\right[_{\mathrm{id}}
. In general I will often simply write it as
u\colon\left]X^{\prime}\right[_{\mathfrak{P}^{\prime}}\rightarrow\left]X\right[_{\mathfrak{P}}
, in a (possibly doomed) attempt to keep notational complexity within reasonable bounds. I will try my best to do this only when there is no possible scope for confusion.

By definition of the structure sheaves on
\left]X^{\prime}\right[_{\mathfrak{P}}
and
\left]X\right[_{\mathfrak{P}}
, if
u=\mathrm{id}
and
f
is a locally closed immersion, the module pullback and sheaf pullback coincide:
f^{*}=f^{-1}
. The extension by zero functor
f_{!}\colon{\bf Sh}(\left]X^{\prime}\right[_{\mathfrak{P}})\rightarrow{\bf Sh}(\left]X^{\prime}\right[_{\mathfrak{P}})
can also be upgraded to a functor of
\mathcal{O}
-modules, which coincides with the functor on underlying sheaves. The following simple ‘base change’ result will be used constantly.

1.2.6 Lemma
.

Let
(f,g,u):(X^{\prime},Y^{\prime},\mathfrak{P}^{\prime})\rightarrow(X,Y,\mathfrak{P})
be a morphism of frames, and
i:S\rightarrow X
a locally closed immersion. Set
S^{\prime}:=f^{-1}(S)
, and write
i^{\prime}:S^{\prime}\rightarrow X^{\prime}
for the induced locally closed immersion. Consider the (topologically Cartesian) diagram

\textstyle{\left]S^{\prime}\right[_{\mathfrak{P}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{i^{\prime}}
\scriptstyle{\left]f\right[_{u}}
\textstyle{\left]X^{\prime}\right[_{\mathfrak{P}^{\prime}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\left]f\right[_{u}}
\textstyle{\left]S\right[_{\mathfrak{P}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{i}
\textstyle{\left]X\right[_{\mathfrak{P}}}

of tubes. Then, for any
\mathcal{O}_{\left]S\right[_{\mathfrak{P}}}
-module
\mathscr{F}
, the base change map

\left]f\right[_{u}^{*}i_{*}\mathscr{F}\rightarrow i^{\prime}_{*}\left]f\right[_{u}^{*}\mathscr{F}

induces an isomorphism

\left]f\right[_{u}^{*}i_{!}\mathscr{F}\overset{\cong}{\longrightarrow}i^{\prime}_{!}\left]f\right[_{u}^{*}\mathscr{F}.

Proof.

Set
T=X\setminus S
and
T^{\prime}=X^{\prime}\setminus S^{\prime}=f^{-1}(T)
and let
j:T\rightarrow X
,
j^{\prime}:T^{\prime}\rightarrow X^{\prime}
be the natural inclusions (of constructible subsets). Then the commutativity of the diagram

\textstyle{\left]T^{\prime}\right[_{\mathfrak{P}^{\prime}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{j^{\prime}}
\scriptstyle{\left]f\right[_{u}}
\textstyle{\left]X^{\prime}\right[_{\mathfrak{P}^{\prime}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\left]f\right[_{u}}
\textstyle{\left]T\right[_{\mathfrak{P}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{j}
\textstyle{\left]X\right[_{\mathfrak{P}}}

shows that
j^{\prime-1}\left]f\right[_{u}^{*}i_{!}\mathscr{F}=\left]f\right[_{u}^{*}j^{-1}i_{!}\mathscr{F}=0
. First of all, this implies that the canonical map
\left]f\right[_{u}^{*}i_{!}\mathscr{F}\rightarrow\left]f\right[_{u}^{*}i_{*}\mathscr{F}\rightarrow i^{\prime}_{*}\left]f\right[_{u}^{*}\mathscr{F}
factors through the subsheaf
i^{\prime}_{!}\left]f\right[_{u}^{*}\mathscr{F}\subset i^{\prime}_{*}\left]f\right[_{u}^{*}\mathscr{F}
. Secondly, since
j^{\prime-1}i^{\prime}_{!}\left]f\right[_{u}^{*}\mathscr{F}=0
trivially, we can see that to prove

\left]f\right[_{u}^{*}i_{!}\mathscr{F}\rightarrow i^{\prime}_{!}\left]f\right[_{u}^{*}\mathscr{F}

is an isomorphism, it suffices to do so after restricting to
\left]S^{\prime}\right[_{\mathfrak{P}^{\prime}}
. But now we have

i^{\prime-1}\left]f\right[_{u}^{*}i_{!}\mathscr{F}=\left]f\right[_{u}^{*}i^{-1}i_{!}\mathscr{F}=\left]f\right[_{u}^{*}\mathscr{F},\;\;\;\;i^{\prime-1}i^{\prime}_{!}\left]f\right[_{u}^{*}\mathscr{F}=\left]f\right[_{u}^{*}\mathscr{F},

and the morphism

\left]f\right[_{u}^{*}i_{!}\mathscr{F}\rightarrow i^{\prime}_{!}\left]f\right[_{u}^{*}\mathscr{F}

restricts to the identity on
\left]S^{\prime}\right[_{\mathfrak{P}^{\prime}}
.
∎

The tube of a subset can be used to formulate the following rather weak notion of smoothness for frames, which will occasionally be useful.

1.2.7 Definition
.

If
(X,Y,\mathfrak{P})
is a frame, I will say that
\mathfrak{P}
is rig-smooth around
X
if
\left]X\right[_{\mathfrak{P}}
admits an open neighbourhood
\left]X\right[_{\mathfrak{P}}\subset V\subset\mathfrak{P}_{K}
which is smooth over
K
.

The non-smooth locus of a morphism of analytic varieties is the subspace defined by a coherent ideal sheaf. Thus, if
\mathfrak{P}
is smooth around
X
, then it is rig-smooth around
X
. It is easy to provide counter-examples to the converse statement.

Finally, as well as the open tube
\left]Y\right[_{\mathfrak{P}}
of a closed subscheme
Y\hookrightarrow P
defined above, I will also need the variants
\left[Y\right]_{\mathfrak{P}\eta}
and
\left]Y\right[_{\mathfrak{P}\eta}
, which are defined for
\eta<1
sufficiently close to
1
. When
\mathfrak{P}
is affine, and
f_{1},\ldots,f_{r}\in\Gamma(\mathfrak{P},\mathcal{O}_{\mathfrak{P}})
are such that
Y=V(\varpi,f_{1},\ldots,f_{r})
, then

\left]Y\right[_{\mathfrak{P}}=\left\{\left.x\in\mathfrak{P}_{K}\right|v_{[x]}(f_{i})<1\;\forall i\right\}

by
[
FK18
, Proposition II.4.2.11]
. Berthelot therefore defines the closed and open tubes

\displaystyle\left[Y\right]_{\mathfrak{P}\eta}

\displaystyle:=\left\{\left.x\in\mathfrak{P}_{K}\right|v_{x}(f_{i})\leq\eta\;\forall i\right\}

\displaystyle\left]Y\right[_{\mathfrak{P}\eta}

\displaystyle:=\left\{\left.x\in\mathfrak{P}_{K}\right|v_{[x]}(f_{i})<\eta\;\forall i\right\}

of radius
\eta
. When
\left|\varpi\right|<\eta<1
these do not depend on the choice of the
f_{i}
, and hence glue together over an affine covering of
\mathfrak{P}
, see
[
Ber96a
, §1.1.8]
.

1.3.
Overconvergence

Berthelot’s functors
j^{\dagger}
and
\underline{\Gamma}^{\dagger}
of overconvergent sections and sections with support have a very natural interpretation in the world of adic spaces. Let
(X,Y,\mathfrak{P})
be a frame, and write
j\colon X\rightarrow Y
for the given open immersion. Let
i\colon Z\rightarrow Y
be a complementary closed immersion.

1.3.1 Definition
.

Define endofunctors

\displaystyle j_{X}^{\dagger}\colon\mathbf{Sh}(\left]Y\right[_{\mathfrak{P}})

\displaystyle\rightarrow\mathbf{Sh}(\left]Y\right[_{\mathfrak{P}})

\displaystyle\underline{\Gamma}^{\dagger}_{Z}\colon\mathbf{Sh}(\left]Y\right[_{\mathfrak{P}})

\displaystyle\rightarrow\mathbf{Sh}(\left]Y\right[_{\mathfrak{P}})

by
j_{X}^{\dagger}:=j_{*}j^{-1}
and
\underline{\Gamma}^{\dagger}_{Z}:=i_{!}i^{-1}
.

These are both exact, and there is an exact sequence

0\rightarrow\underline{\Gamma}^{\dagger}_{Z}\rightarrow\mathrm{id}\rightarrow j_{X}^{\dagger}\rightarrow 0

of endofunctors of
\mathbf{Sh}(\left]Y\right[_{\mathfrak{P}})
. More generally, if
j^{\prime}\colon U\rightarrow X\leftarrow T\colon i^{\prime}
are complementary open and closed immersions, then we have functors

\displaystyle j_{U}^{\dagger}

\displaystyle:=j^{\prime}_{*}j^{\prime-1}\colon\mathbf{Sh}(\left]X\right[_{\mathfrak{P}})\rightarrow\mathbf{Sh}(\left]X\right[_{\mathfrak{P}})

\displaystyle\underline{\Gamma}^{\dagger}_{T}

\displaystyle:=i^{\prime}_{!}i^{\prime-1}\colon\mathbf{Sh}(\left]X\right[_{\mathfrak{P}})\rightarrow\mathbf{Sh}(\left]X\right[_{\mathfrak{P}})

sitting in a short exact sequence

0\rightarrow\underline{\Gamma}^{\dagger}_{T}\rightarrow\mathrm{id}\rightarrow j_{U}^{\dagger}\rightarrow 0.

To compare these with the original definitions of Berthelot, let
\mathfrak{P}_{K0}
denote the rigid analytic generic fibre of
\mathfrak{P}
in the sense of
[
Ber96a
, §0.2]
. In the notation of §
1.1
above this is the rigid analytic space
(\mathfrak{P}_{K})_{0}
. Let
\left]Y\right[_{\mathfrak{P}0}\subset\mathfrak{P}_{K0}
denote the rigid analytic tube in the sense of Berthelot
[
Ber96a
, §1.1]
, and
j_{X}^{\dagger}
,
\underline{\Gamma}^{\dagger}_{Z}
the corresponding endofunctors of
\mathbf{Sh}_{G}(\left]Y\right[_{\mathfrak{P}0})
as defined in
[
Ber96a
, §2.1]
.

1.3.2 Proposition
.

There is a unique isomorphism
\left(\left]Y\right[_{\mathfrak{P}}\right)_{0}\overset{\cong}{\longrightarrow}\left]Y\right[_{\mathfrak{P}0}
of rigid analytic spaces over
K
, compatible with the natural open immersions of both sides into
\mathfrak{P}_{K0}
. Moreover, the diagrams

\textstyle{\mathbf{Sh}(\left]Y\right[_{\mathfrak{P}})\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{(-)_{0}}
\scriptstyle{j_{X}^{\dagger}}
\textstyle{\mathbf{Sh}_{G}(\left]Y\right[_{\mathfrak{P}0})\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{j_{X}^{\dagger}}
\textstyle{\mathbf{Sh}(\left]Y\right[_{\mathfrak{P}})\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{(-)_{0}}
\scriptstyle{\underline{\Gamma}^{\dagger}_{Z}}
\textstyle{\mathbf{Sh}_{G}(\left]Y\right[_{\mathfrak{P}0})\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\underline{\Gamma}^{\dagger}_{Z}}
\textstyle{\mathbf{Sh}(\left]Y\right[_{\mathfrak{P}})\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{(-)_{0}}
\textstyle{\mathbf{Sh}_{G}(\left]Y\right[_{\mathfrak{P}0})}
\textstyle{\mathbf{Sh}(\left]Y\right[_{\mathfrak{P}})\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{(-)_{0}}
\textstyle{\mathbf{Sh}_{G}(\left]Y\right[_{\mathfrak{P}0})}

commute up to natural isomorphism.

Proof.

Note that
\mathfrak{P}_{K0}
can be identified with the set of
rigid
points of
\mathfrak{P}_{K}
, and the functor
U\mapsto U\cap\mathfrak{P}_{K0}
gives a one-to-one correspondence between admissible open subsets of
\mathfrak{P}_{K}
(in the sense of
[
FK18
, Definition II.B.1.1]
) and admissible open subsets of
\mathfrak{P}_{K0}
(in the sense of the
G
-topology). Since tube open subsets of
\mathfrak{P}
are admissible by
[
FK18
, Proposition II.B.1.7]
, the first claim is reduced to showing that
\left]Y\right[_{\mathfrak{P}}\cap\mathfrak{P}_{K0}=\left]Y\right[_{\mathfrak{P}0}
as subsets of
\mathfrak{P}
. The question is now local on
\mathfrak{P}
, which we may assume to be affine. Let
f_{1},\ldots,f_{r}\in\Gamma(\mathfrak{P},\mathcal{O}_{\mathfrak{P}})
be such that
Y=V(\varpi,f_{1},\ldots,f_{r})
. Then by
[
FK18
, Proposition II.4.2.11]
we can identify

\left]Y\right[_{\mathfrak{P}}=\left\{\left.x\in\mathfrak{P}_{K}\right|v_{[x]}(f_{i})<1\;\forall i\right\},

where
[x]
denote the maximal generalisation of
x
. Since rigid points
x
satisfy
x=[x]
, the claim now reduces to
[
Ber96a
, Proposition 1.1.1]
. For the second claim, there are exact sequences

0\rightarrow\underline{\Gamma}^{\dagger}_{Z}\rightarrow\mathrm{id}\rightarrow j_{X}^{\dagger}\rightarrow 0

of functors on both
\mathbf{Sh}(\left]Y\right[_{\mathfrak{P}})
and
\mathbf{Sh}_{G}(\left]Y\right[_{\mathfrak{P}0})
, it therefore suffices to consider
j_{X}^{\dagger}
. In this case, since the topos of an analytic variety is equivalent to that of the associated rigid space, the claim fact follows from the alternative definition of
j_{X}^{\dagger}
given in
[
LS07
, Proposition 5.3]
(for the equivalence with Berthelot’s definition, see
[
LS07
, Proposition 5.1.12]
).
∎

On the level of
\mathcal{O}
-modules, Proposition
1.3.2
shows that:

(1)

there is a canonical isomorphism
\left(j_{X}^{\dagger}\mathcal{O}_{\left]Y\right[_{\mathfrak{P}}}\right)_{0}\cong j_{X}^{\dagger}\mathcal{O}_{\left]Y\right[_{\mathfrak{P}0}}
;

(2)

the functor
\mathscr{F}\mapsto\mathscr{F}_{0}
induces an equivalence of categories

\mathbf{Mod}(j_{X}^{\dagger}\mathcal{O}_{\left]Y\right[_{\mathfrak{P}}})\overset{\cong}{\longrightarrow}\mathbf{Mod}(j_{X}^{\dagger}\mathcal{O}_{\left]Y\right[_{\mathfrak{P}0}}),

preserving the full subcategories of coherent modules.

We can then use this to transport all results proved for overconvergent sheaves in the language of rigid analytic spaces into the adic context, for example the following.

1.3.3 Proposition

(Proposition 2.1.10,
[
Ber96a
]
)
.

The inverse image functor induces an equivalence of categories

\varinjlim_{V}\mathbf{Coh}(\mathcal{O}_{V})\overset{\cong}{\longrightarrow}\mathbf{Coh}(\mathcal{O}_{\left]X\right[_{\mathfrak{P}}})

where
V
ranges over all open neighbourhoods of
\left]X\right[_{\mathfrak{P}}
in
\left]Y\right[_{\mathfrak{P}}
.

In particular, this implies that
\mathcal{O}_{\left]X\right[_{\mathfrak{P}}}
and
j_{X}^{\dagger}\mathcal{O}_{\left]Y\right[_{\mathfrak{P}}}
are coherent sheaves of rings on
\left]X\right[_{\mathfrak{P}}
and
\left]Y\right[_{\mathfrak{P}}
respectively. Since
j\colon\left]X\right[_{\mathfrak{P}}\rightarrow\left]Y\right[_{\mathfrak{P}}
is a closed immersion on the underling topological spaces, the following lemma is then elementary.

1.3.4 Lemma
.

The functors

j_{*}\colon\mathbf{Coh}(\mathcal{O}_{\left]X\right[_{\mathfrak{P}}})\leftrightarrows\mathbf{Coh}(j_{X}^{\dagger}\mathcal{O}_{\left]Y\right[_{\mathfrak{P}}})\colon j^{-1}

are inverse equivalences of categories. If
\mathscr{E}
is a coherent
j_{X}^{\dagger}\mathcal{O}_{\left]Y\right[_{\mathfrak{P}}}
-module, then

{\rm H}^{*}(\left]Y\right[_{\mathfrak{P}},\mathscr{E})\overset{\cong}{\longrightarrow}{\rm H}^{*}(\left]X\right[_{\mathfrak{P}},j^{-1}\mathscr{E}).

1.4.
Germs and compactly supported de Rham cohomology

If
(X,Y,\mathfrak{P})
is a frame, then the tube
\left]X\right[_{\mathfrak{P}}
is not an adic space, but a closed subset of the adic space
\left]Y\right[_{\mathfrak{P}}
. Moreover, it has the property that it is stable under generalisation inside
\left]Y\right[_{\mathfrak{P}}
, in other words it is an
overconvergent
closed subset. In our previous work on rigid cohomology and arithmetic
\mathscr{D}
-modules
[
AL20
,
AL22
]
, we found it useful to formalise this in the notion of an
overconvergent germ
.

1.4.1 Definition
.

(1)

A pre-germ is a pair
(S,\mathscr{X})
where
\mathscr{X}
is an analytic variety, and
S\subset\mathscr{X}
is a closed subset.

(2)

A morphism
f\colon(S,\mathscr{X})\rightarrow(T,\mathscr{Y})
of pre-germs is a morphism
f\colon\mathscr{X}\rightarrow\mathscr{Y}
of analytic varieties such that
f(S)\subset T
.

(3)

A morphism
f\colon(S,\mathscr{X})\rightarrow(T,\mathscr{Y})
of pre-germs is called a strict neighbourhood if
f\colon\mathscr{X}\hookrightarrow\mathscr{Y}
is an open immersion inducing a homeomorphism
f\colon S\overset{\cong}{\longrightarrow}T
.

(4)

The category of germs of analytic varieties is the localisation of the category of pre-germs at the class of strict neighbourhoods.

(5)

A germ
(S,\mathscr{X})
is called
overconvergent
if
S
is stable under generalisation in
\mathscr{X}
.

I will generally suppress the ambient adic space
\mathscr{X}
from the notation, and write a germ
(S,\mathscr{X})
simply as
S
. Thus the motivating example
(\left]X\right[_{\mathfrak{P}},\left]Y\right[_{\mathfrak{P}})
of an overconvergent germ will be written simply as
\left]X\right[_{\mathfrak{P}}
. The category of germs admits fibre products, and contains the category of analytic varieties as a full subcategory. Thus it is possible to form spaces such as the relative (open or closed) polydisc
{\mathbb{D}}^{r}_{S}(0;\rho^{(-)})
over a germ
S
. For example, in the language of germs, Berthelot’s strong fibration theorem
[
Ber96a
]
has the following form.

1.4.2 Theorem

(Berthelot)
.

Let
(f,g,u)\colon(X^{\prime},Y^{\prime},\mathfrak{P}^{\prime})\rightarrow(X,Y,\mathfrak{P})
be a morphism of frames, such that
f
is an isomorphism,
g
is proper and
u
is smooth in a neighbourhood of
X^{\prime}
, of relative dimension
d
.

(1)

If
g
is also an isomorphism, then, locally on
X
and on
\mathfrak{P}
, there exists an isomorphism

\left]X^{\prime}\right[_{\mathfrak{P}^{\prime}}\overset{\cong}{\longrightarrow}\left]X\right[_{\mathfrak{P}}\times_{K}{\mathbb{D}}^{d}_{K}(0;1^{-})

of germs, identifying
\left]f\right[_{u}
with the first projection.

(2)

If
d=0
, then

\left]f\right[_{u}\colon\left]X^{\prime}\right[_{\mathfrak{P}^{\prime}}\overset{\cong}{\longrightarrow}\left]X\right[_{\mathfrak{P}}

is an isomorphism of germs.

As with the case of tubes, any germ
S
can be viewed as a ringed space by equipping it with the restriction

\mathcal{O}_{S}:=\mathcal{O}_{\mathscr{X}}\!|_{S}

of the structure sheaf from its ambient adic space. Similarly, if
f\colon S\rightarrow T
is a morphism in
\mathbf{Germ}_{K}
, the relative de Rham complex
\Omega^{\bullet}_{S/T}
is defined via restriction from the ambient adic space.

I will occasionally talk about morphisms of germs being smooth or partially proper, or other similar adjectives familiar from the theory of schemes and/or adic spaces. This should always be understood in the sense of
[
Hub96
, §1.10]
. For example, being smooth means that there exists a representative
f\colon(S,\mathscr{X})\rightarrow(T,\mathscr{Y})
at the level of pre-germs such that
f\colon\mathscr{X}\rightarrow\mathscr{Y}
is smooth and
S=f^{-1}(T)
. Partial properness is slightly more involved, see
[
Hub96
, Definition 1.10.15]
. In fact, more important for us than partial properness is what might be called partial properness in the sense of Kiehl. This is a morphism
f\colon S\rightarrow T
which, locally on the source and target, factors through a closed immersion
S\rightarrow{\mathbb{D}}^{d}_{T}(0;1^{-})
for some
d
. For a more detailed discussion of this property, see
[
AL20
, §4]
.

Note that being smooth in this sense is a very strong condition on a morphism of germs. For example, if
S
is a germ which is
not
an open subset of its ambient adic space, then the structure morphism
f\colon S\rightarrow\mathrm{Spa}\left(K,\mathcal{V}\right)
cannot be smooth. In particular, if
(X,Y,\mathfrak{P})
is a frame, then
\left]X\right[_{\mathfrak{P}}
cannot be smooth over
K
if
X
is not closed in
Y
. It will therefore be helpful to make the following weakening of the definition.

1.4.3 Definition
.

A morphism
f\colon(S,\mathscr{X})\rightarrow(T,\mathscr{Y})
of germs is
quasi-smooth
if there exists an open neighbourhood
S\subset V\subset\mathscr{X}
which is smooth over
\mathscr{Y}
.

Note that this only depends on the induced morphism of germs, and implies that
\Omega_{S/T}
is a locally finite free
\mathcal{O}_{S}
-module.

1.4.4 Example
.

If
(X,Y,\mathfrak{P})
is a frame, then
\left]X\right[_{\mathfrak{P}}
is quasi-smooth over
K
if and only if
\mathfrak{P}
is rig-smooth around
X
.

More generally, let
(f,g,u)\colon(X^{\prime},Y^{\prime},\mathfrak{P}^{\prime})\rightarrow(X,Y,\mathfrak{P})
be a morphism of frames. If
g
is proper, then
\left]f\right[_{u}\colon\left]X^{\prime}\right[_{\mathfrak{P}^{\prime}}\rightarrow\left]X\right[_{\mathfrak{P}}
is partially proper in the sense of Kiehl. Indeed, the morphism
\left]Y^{\prime}\right[_{\mathfrak{P}^{\prime}}\rightarrow\left]Y\right[_{\mathfrak{P}}
is a partially proper morphism of analytic varieties, and is hence partially proper in the sense of Kiehl by
[
Hub96
, Remark 1.3.19]
, and
\left]X^{\prime}\right[_{\mathfrak{P}^{\prime}}\rightarrow\left]Y^{\prime}\right[_{\mathfrak{P}^{\prime}}
is a closed immersion.

If
u
is smooth in a neighbourhood of
X^{\prime}
, then
\left]f\right[_{u}
is quasi-smooth, because the non-smooth locus of
\left]g\right[_{u}
is a closed analytic subspace of
\left]Y^{\prime}\right[_{\mathfrak{P}^{\prime}}
. If, moreover, the given square

\textstyle{X^{\prime}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{f}
\textstyle{Y^{\prime}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{g}
\textstyle{X\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{Y}

of varieties is Cartesian, then
\left]X^{\prime}\right[_{\mathfrak{P}^{\prime}}=\left]g\right[_{u}^{-1}(\left]X\right[_{\mathfrak{P}})
, and so
\left]f\right[_{u}
is smooth. Up to possibly replacing
Y^{\prime}
by a closed subscheme containing
X^{\prime}
, the above square will be Cartesian whenever both
g
and
f
are proper.

Quasi-smooth germs also satisfy analytic continuation.

1.4.5 Lemma
.

Let
S
be a connected, quasi-smooth germ, and
\mathscr{F}
a locally finite free
\mathcal{O}_{S}
-module. Then, for any non-empty open subset
U\subset S
, the natural map

\Gamma(S,\mathscr{F})\rightarrow\Gamma(U,\mathscr{F})

is injective.

Proof.

Fix a smooth ambient analytic space
\mathscr{X}
for
S
, and let
s\in\Gamma(S,\mathscr{F})
be a section. If
U,V\subset S
are open subsets such that
s|_{U}=0
and
s|_{V}=0
, then
s|_{U\cup V}=0
. We may therefore take
U
maximal with the property that
s|_{U}=0
.

Suppose for contradiction that
U\neq S
. Consider the collection
\mathcal{C}
of all connected open subsets
V\subset S
such that:

•

V=\bm{V}\cap S
for a connected open
\bm{V}\subset\mathscr{X}
;

•

\mathscr{F}|_{V}\cong\mathcal{O}_{V}^{\oplus n}
where
n=\mathrm{rank}\,\mathscr{F}
;

•

the section
s|_{V}
of
\mathscr{F}|_{V}\cong\mathcal{O}_{V}^{\oplus n}
extends to a section of
\Gamma(\bm{V},\mathcal{O}_{\bm{V}}^{\oplus n})
.

Note that every point
x\in S
admits a cofinal set of neighbourhoods satisfying the first and second of these, any sufficiently small element of which also satisfies the third. Hence
x
at least one neighbourhood satisfying all three. Therefore, such open sets cover
S
, and since
S
is connected, we deduce that there exists some
V\in\mathcal{C}
such that
V\not\subset U
and
U\cap V\neq\emptyset
. To contradict the maximality of
U
, it therefore suffices to show that
s|_{V}=0
. But we know that
s|_{U\cap V}=0
, and since
s|_{V}
extends to
\bm{V}
, it suffices to show that

\Gamma(\bm{V},\mathcal{O}_{\bm{V}})\rightarrow\Gamma(U\cap V,\mathcal{O}_{\bm{V}}).

If we pick any point
v\in U\cap V
, it therefore suffices to show that

\Gamma(\bm{V},\mathcal{O}_{\bm{V}})\rightarrow\mathcal{O}_{\bm{V},v}

is injective. Since
\bm{V}
is smooth and connected, this follows from
[
Ber96a
, Proposition 0.1.13]
.
∎

For any partially proper morphism of germs
f\colon S\rightarrow T
, we defined in
[
AL20
]
a functor

\mathbf{R}f_{!}\colon{\bf D}^{+}(S)\rightarrow{\bf D}^{+}(T),

having the following properties:

(1)

f_{!}:=\mathcal{H}^{0}(\mathbf{R}f_{!})
is the functor of sections with proper support;

(2)

\mathbf{R}f_{!}
is the total derived functor of
f_{!}
;

(3)

there is a natural isomorphism
\mathbf{R}g_{!}\circ\mathbf{R}f_{!}\overset{\cong}{\longrightarrow}\mathbf{R}(g\circ f)_{!}
whenever
f,g
are composable partially proper morphisms;

(4)

\mathbf{R}f_{!}=\mathbf{R}f_{*}
whenever
f
is proper;

(5)

\mathbf{R}f_{!}=f_{!}
is the usual extension by zero functor whenever
f
is a (partially proper) locally closed immersion.

If
f:S\rightarrow\mathrm{Spa}\left(K,\mathcal{V}\right)
is the structure map of a germ, I will write
\mathbf{R}\Gamma_{c}(S,-)
for
\mathbf{R}f_{!}
, and
{\rm H}^{i}_{c}(S,-)
for its cohomology groups.

The functor
\mathbf{R}f_{!}
preserves module structures, and there is the following version of the projection formula.

1.4.6 Lemma

(
[
AL20
]
, Corollary 3.8.2)
.

Let
f:S\rightarrow T
be a partially proper morphism of germs. For any locally free
\mathcal{O}_{T}
-module
\mathscr{F}
of finite rank, and any complex
\mathscr{G}
of
\mathcal{O}_{S}
-modules, there is a natural isomorphism

\mathscr{F}\otimes_{\mathcal{O}_{T}}\mathbf{R}f_{!}\mathscr{G}\overset{\cong}{\longrightarrow}\mathbf{R}f_{!}(f^{*}\mathscr{F}\otimes_{\mathcal{O}_{S}}\mathscr{G})

in
{\bf D}^{+}(\mathcal{O}_{T})
.

In general, the proper base change theorem for
\mathbf{R}f_{!}
fails, but there is at least the following partial result.

1.4.7 Proposition

(
[
AL20
]
, Lemma 3.5.2)
.

Let

\textstyle{S^{\prime}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{g^{\prime}}
\scriptstyle{f^{\prime}}
\textstyle{S\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{f}
\textstyle{T^{\prime}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{g}
\textstyle{T}

by a Cartesian diagram of germs, such that
f
is partially proper, and
g
is one of the following:

(1)

a locally closed immersion onto a subspace which is closed under generalisation;

(2)

the inclusion of a maximal point of
T
.

Then, for any
\mathscr{F}\in{\bf D}^{+}(S)
, the base change map

g^{-1}\mathbf{R}f_{!}\mathscr{F}\rightarrow\mathbf{R}f^{\prime}_{!}g^{\prime-1}\mathscr{F}

is an isomorphism.

If
f\colon S\rightarrow T
is a quasi-smooth morphism of germs, de Rham pushforwards with or without proper supports can be defined in the usual way. Namely, if
\mathscr{F}
is an
\mathcal{O}_{S}
-module with integrable connection, then we form the de Rham complex
\Omega^{\bullet}_{S/T}\otimes_{\mathcal{O}_{S}}\mathscr{F}
and set

\displaystyle{\bf R}f_{\mathrm{dR}*}\mathscr{F}

\displaystyle:={\bf R}f_{*}(\Omega^{\bullet}_{S/T}\otimes_{\mathcal{O}_{S}}\mathscr{F})

\displaystyle{\bf R}f_{\mathrm{dR}!}\mathscr{F}

\displaystyle:={\bf R}f_{!}(\Omega^{\bullet}_{S/T}\otimes_{\mathcal{O}_{S}}\mathscr{F}).

as objects of
{\bf D}^{+}(\mathcal{O}_{T})
. If, moreover,
T
is quasi-smooth over
K
, then these can be upgraded to (complexes of) modules over the ring of differential operators on
T
. Indeed, if
\mathscr{X}
and
\mathscr{Y}
are ambient analytic varieties for
S
and
T
respectively, then we set

\mathscr{D}_{T}:=\mathscr{D}_{\mathscr{Y}}\!\!\mid_{T},\;\;\;\;\mathscr{D}_{S}:=\mathscr{D}_{\mathscr{X}}\!\!\mid_{S}.

The transfer bimodule
\mathscr{D}_{T\leftarrow S}:=\mathscr{D}_{\mathscr{Y}\leftarrow\mathscr{X}}\!\!\mid_{S}
is also defined via restriction, and there is the usual identification

\Omega^{\bullet}_{S/T}\otimes_{\mathcal{O}_{S}}\mathscr{F}\overset{\cong}{\longrightarrow}\mathscr{D}_{T\leftarrow S}\otimes^{\bf L}_{\mathscr{D}_{S}}\mathscr{F}[-\dim f].

The left
\mathscr{D}_{T}
-module structure on
\mathscr{D}_{T\leftarrow S}
therefore upgrades
{\bf R}f_{\mathrm{dR}*}\mathscr{F}
and
{\bf R}f_{\mathrm{dR}!}\mathscr{F}
to complexes of
\mathscr{D}_{T}
-modules.

1.5.
The trace morphism

Now suppose that
f\colon S\rightarrow T
is a smooth morphism of germs,
10
10

10

recall that this is a very strong condition on
f
!
of relative dimension
d
, and partially proper in the sense of Kiehl. If
T
is overconvergent, we constructed in
[
AL20
, §5]
a trace map

\mathrm{Tr}\colon\mathbf{R}^{2d}f_{\mathrm{dR}!}\mathcal{O}_{S}\rightarrow\mathcal{O}_{T}

having the following properties:

(1)

if
T=\mathrm{Spa}\left(R,R^{+}\right)
is affinoid, and
S={\mathbb{D}}^{d}_{Y}(0;1^{-})
is the relative open unit disc over
Y
, with co-ordinates
z_{1},\ldots,z_{d}
, then
\mathrm{Tr}
is induced by the residue map

\displaystyle{\rm H}^{d}_{c}(S/T,\omega_{S/T})=R\langle z_{1}^{-1},\ldots,z_{d}^{-1}\rangle^{\dagger}\;d\log z_{1}\wedge\ldots\wedge d\log z_{d}

\displaystyle\rightarrow R

\displaystyle\sum_{i_{1},\ldots,i_{d}\geq 0}r_{i_{1},\ldots,i_{d}}z_{1}^{-i_{1}}\ldots z_{d}^{-i_{d}}\;d\log z_{1}\wedge\ldots\wedge d\log z_{d}

\displaystyle\mapsto r_{0,\ldots,0};

(2)

whenever
S
is locally either a
{\mathbb{D}}^{d}(0;1^{-})
-bundle or an
{\mathbb{A}}^{d,\mathrm{an}}
-bundle over
T
,
\mathrm{Tr}
is an isomorphism.

Moreover, we showed that
\mathbf{R}^{q}f_{\mathrm{dR}!}\mathcal{O}_{S}=0
if
q>2d
, and hence the trace map can be viewed as a morphism

\mathrm{Tr}\colon\mathbf{R}f_{\mathrm{dR}!}\mathcal{O}_{S}[2d]\rightarrow\mathcal{O}_{T}.

The projection formula then gives rise to a trace map

\mathrm{Tr}_{\mathscr{F}}\colon\mathbf{R}f_{\mathrm{dR}!}f^{*}\mathscr{F}[2d]=\mathbf{R}f_{\mathrm{dR}!}\mathcal{O}_{S}\otimes_{\mathcal{O}_{T}}\mathscr{F}[2d]\rightarrow\mathscr{F}

for any finite locally free
\mathcal{O}_{T}
-module
\mathscr{F}
. Whenever
\mathscr{F}
has the structure of a
\mathscr{D}_{T}
-module, this map is
\mathscr{D}_{T}
-linear, and whenever
S
is locally either a
{\mathbb{D}}^{d}(0;1^{-})
-bundle or an
{\mathbb{A}}^{d,\mathrm{an}}
-bundle over
T
, this map is an isomorphism.

1.5.1 Corollary
.

Let
(f,g,u)\colon(X^{\prime},Y^{\prime},\mathfrak{P}^{\prime})\rightarrow(X,Y,\mathfrak{P})
be a morphism of frames, such that
f
is an isomorphism,
g
is proper and
u
is smooth in a neighbourhood of
X^{\prime}
, of relative dimension
d
. Then the trace map

\mathrm{Tr}\colon\mathbf{R}\!\left]f\right[_{\mathrm{dR}!}\mathcal{O}_{\left]X^{\prime}\right[_{\mathfrak{P}^{\prime}}}[2d]\rightarrow\mathcal{O}_{\left]X\right[_{\mathfrak{P}}}

is an isomorphism in
{\bf D}^{b}(\mathscr{D}_{\left]X\right[_{\mathfrak{P}}})
.

Proof.

This follows from Theorem
1.4.2
in exactly the same way as the proof that

\mathcal{O}_{\left]X\right[_{\mathfrak{P}}}\rightarrow\mathbf{R}\!\left]f\right[_{\mathrm{dR}*}\mathcal{O}_{\left]X^{\prime}\right[_{\mathfrak{P}^{\prime}}}

is an isomorphism, see for example
[
LS07
, §6.5]
. The first step is to show the result is true when
g
is an isomorphism, which is a fairly direct consequence of Theorem
1.4.2
and the above properties of the trace map. This then implies that the claim only depends on the given morphism of pairs
(X,Y^{\prime})\rightarrow(X,Y)
and not on the ambient morphism
u
of formal schemes.

The question is then local on
Y
, which can therefore be assumed quasi-projective, and one can use Chow’s lemma to find a projective morphism
Y^{\prime\prime}\rightarrow Y^{\prime}
such that
Y^{\prime\prime}\rightarrow Y
is also projective. Via a two-out-of-three argument, it therefore suffices to prove the result for both
(X,Y^{\prime\prime})\rightarrow(X,Y^{\prime})
and
(X,Y^{\prime\prime})\rightarrow(X,Y)
. This therefore reduces to the case when
g\colon Y^{\prime}\rightarrow Y
is projective.

Proposition
1.4.7
together with Berthelot’s resolution
[
Ber96a
, Proposition 2.1.8]
then implies that the question is local on
X
, and thanks to
[
LS07
, Lemma 6.5.1]
one can then assume that
u
is actually étale in a neighbourhood of
X
. Finally, this case follows directly from Theorem
1.4.2
.
∎

1.6.
Sheaves on diagrams of spaces

To make precise certain constructions, involving derived functors applied to diagrams of complexes, I will need to use the formalism of diagram toposes. So let
I
be a category, and
X\colon I\rightarrow{\bf Top}
a diagram in the category of topological spaces. Then there is a natural site
X_{\rm Zar}
associated to
X
whose objects are pairs
(i,U)
where
i\in I
and
U\subset X_{i}
is an open subset, and morphisms
(i,U)\rightarrow(j,V)
consist of morphisms
\alpha\colon i\rightarrow j
such that the morphism
\alpha\colon X_{i}\rightarrow X_{j}
satisfies
U\subset\alpha^{-1}(V)
. A covering family is just a covering family on a single
X_{i}
. Sheaves on this site have the usual description as sheaves
\mathscr{F}_{i}
on each
X_{i}
, together with transition maps
\alpha^{-1}\mathscr{F}_{j}\rightarrow\mathscr{F}_{i}
for any
\alpha\colon i\rightarrow j
in
I
, satisfying the usual compatibility condition for commutative triangles in
I
. The sheaf
\mathscr{F}_{i}
is called the restriction of
\mathscr{F}
to
X_{i}
. I will usually abuse terminology and talk above sheaves on the diagram
X
itself.

1.6.1 Example
.

(1)

If
X
is a diagram of ringed spaces, then
X_{\rm Zar}
is naturally ringed by the sheaf of rings
\mathcal{O}_{X}
whose restriction to each
X_{i}
is
\mathcal{O}_{X,i}:=\mathcal{O}_{X_{i}}
.

(2)

If
X
is a constant diagram, then the category of sheaves on
X
is equivalent to the category of
I^{\rm op}
-shaped diagrams of sheaves on the single topological space
X
.

Now suppose that
Y\colon J\rightarrow{\bf Top}
is another diagram of topological spaces. Suppose furthermore that there is a functor
f^{-1}\colon J\rightarrow I
, together with a morphism
f\colon X\circ f^{-1}\rightarrow Y
of
J
-shaped diagrams in
{\bf Top}
. There is then a natural functor

\displaystyle f^{-1}\colon Y_{\rm Zar}

\displaystyle\rightarrow X_{\rm Zar}

\displaystyle(j,V)

\displaystyle\mapsto(f^{-1}(j),f^{-1}(V)).

Explicitly, if
(j,V)\in Y_{\rm Zar}
, with
V\subset Y_{j}
, then
f\colon X_{f^{-1}(j)}\rightarrow Y_{j}
, and thus
f^{-1}(V)\subset X_{f^{-1}(j)}
.

1.6.2 Lemma
.

Assume that for every
i\in I
, the category
i/J
is cofiltered. Then
f^{-1}
induces a morphism of sites
f\colon X_{\rm Zar}\rightarrow Y_{\rm Zar}
.

Proof.

The functor
f^{-1}
induces, by the usual formulae, a functor
f^{-1}
from sheaves on
Y_{\rm Zar}
to those on
X_{\rm Zar}
, and the content of the lemma is that this functor is exact.

Explicitly, if
\mathscr{F}
is a sheaf on
Y_{\rm Zar}
, and
i\in I
, then for any
j\in i/J
there is the given morphism
f_{j}\colon X_{i}\rightarrow Y_{j}
, and the restriction of
f^{-1}\mathscr{F}
to
X_{i}
is given explicitly by
\underset{(i/J)^{\rm op}}{\mathrm{colim}}\,f_{j}^{-1}\mathscr{F}_{j}
. (This can be seen by observing that the functor thus defined is adjoint to
f_{*}
.) If each
(i/J)^{\rm op}
is filtered, then colimits of diagrams indexed by
(i/J)^{\rm op}
are exact, and thus
f^{-1}
is exact as required.
∎

Of course, if
X
and
Y
are diagrams of ringed spaces, and
f\colon X\circ f^{-1}\rightarrow Y
is a morphism of diagrams of ringed spaces, then
f
can be naturally promoted to a morphism of ringed sites (at least under the assumptions of Lemma
1.6.2
.

1.6.3 Example
.

Suppose that
I=\{*\}
is the category with a single object and morphism. Then a morphism
f\colon X\rightarrow Y
amounts to a compatible system of morphisms
f_{j}\colon X\rightarrow Y_{j}
for each
j
. The condition of Lemma
1.6.2
amounts to saying that
J
is cofiltered. In this case, if
\mathscr{F}=\{\mathscr{F}_{j}\}_{j\in J}
is a sheaf on
Y
, then
f^{-1}\mathscr{F}=\underset{J^{\rm op}}{\mathrm{colim}}\,f_{j}^{-1}\mathscr{F}_{j}
.

It is also possible to form morphisms of toposes by taking limits, although of course this doesn’t fit into the general formalism of Lemma
1.6.2
. To describe this construction, let
X
be a topological space,
I
a filtered category, and
X_{I}\colon I\rightarrow{\bf Top}
the constant diagram with value
X
. Thus a sheaf on
X_{I}
is just an
I^{\rm op}
-shaped diagram of sheaves on
X
. The functor

\displaystyle\mathbf{Sh}(X_{I})

\displaystyle\rightarrow\mathbf{Sh}(X)

\displaystyle\{\mathscr{F}_{i}\}_{i\in I}

\displaystyle\mapsto\underset{I}{\mathrm{lim}}\,\mathscr{F}_{i}

is then the pushforward for a morphism of toposes
X_{I}\rightarrow X
. The adjoint pullback functor

\displaystyle\mathbf{Sh}(X)

\displaystyle\rightarrow\mathbf{Sh}(X_{I})

is, of course, the ‘constant diagram’ functor.

1.7.
Limits and colimits

For want of a better place to put them, I prove here a couple of simple lemmas on limits and colimits that will be useful later on.

1.7.1 Lemma
.

Let
A,B:I\rightarrow\mathbf{Ab}
be filtered diagrams of abelian groups, and write
B^{\mathbb{N}}
for the diagram given by
i\mapsto B(i)^{{\mathbb{N}}}:=\prod_{n\in{\mathbb{N}}}B(i)
. Suppose that we are given a morphism

F:A\rightarrow B^{{\mathbb{N}}}

of diagrams. Assume that:

•

F
is level-wise injective;

•

for each
i\in I
, the kernels of the transition maps
B(i)\rightarrow B(j)
for
j\geq i
eventually stabilise.
11
11

11

More formally, for each
i\in I
, there exists some
j_{0}\geq i
, such that for all
j\geq j_{0}
, the inclusion
\ker\left(B(i)\rightarrow B(j_{0})\right)\rightarrow\ker\left(B(i)\rightarrow B(j)\right)
is an equality.

Then the natural map

\mathrm{colim}_{I}A\rightarrow(\mathrm{colim}_{I}B)^{\mathbb{N}}

is also injective.

Proof.

Since
F
is levelwise injective, we see that the map

\mathrm{colim}_{I}A\rightarrow\mathrm{colim}_{I}(B^{{\mathbb{N}}})

is injective, it therefore suffices to prove that the natural map

\mathrm{colim}_{I}(B^{{\mathbb{N}}})\rightarrow(\mathrm{colim}_{I}B)^{{\mathbb{N}}}

is injective. An element of
\mathrm{colim}_{I}(B^{{\mathbb{N}}})
is represented by an element
i\in I
and a collection
(b_{n})\in B(i)^{{\mathbb{N}}}
. Such an element mapping to zero in
(\mathrm{colim}_{I}B)^{{\mathbb{N}}}
says that for each
n
, there exists
i_{n}\geq i
such that
b_{n}\in\ker\left(B(i)\rightarrow B(i_{n})\right)
. The hypothesis that the kernels eventually stabilise means that I can pick a single
j\in I
such that
b_{n}\in\ker\left(B(i)\rightarrow B(j)\right)
for all
n
, thus
(b_{n})\in\ker\left(B(i)^{{\mathbb{N}}}\rightarrow B(j)^{{\mathbb{N}}}\right)
. This implies that
(b_{n})
represents the zero element in
\mathrm{colim}_{I}(B^{{\mathbb{N}}})
, and so

\mathrm{colim}_{I}(B^{{\mathbb{N}}})\rightarrow(\mathrm{colim}_{I}B)^{{\mathbb{N}}}

is injective as claimed.
∎

1.7.2 Lemma
.

Let
X
be a topological space, and
\mathscr{K}_{n}
a uniformly bounded
{\mathbb{N}}
-indexed projective system of complexes on
X
. Suppose that for all
q\in{\mathbb{Z}},n\in{\mathbb{N}}
, the transition maps in cohomology
\mathcal{H}^{q}(\mathscr{K}_{n+1})\rightarrow\mathcal{H}^{q}(\mathscr{K}_{n})
are zero. Then
\mathbf{R}\underset{n}{\mathrm{lim}}\,\mathscr{K}_{n}=0
.

Proof.

By writing
\mathscr{K}_{\bullet}
as an iterated extension of its cohomology sheaves, I can assume that in fact
\mathscr{K}_{\bullet}
is concentrated in a single degree. The assumption that the transition maps
\mathscr{K}_{n+1}\rightarrow\mathscr{K}_{n}
are zero implies that the natural morphism of projective systems

\mathscr{K}_{\bullet+1}\rightarrow\mathscr{K}_{\bullet}

factors through the zero projective system. It follows that the identity map on
\mathbf{R}\underset{n}{\mathrm{lim}}\,\mathscr{K}_{n}
factors through the zero map, and so
\mathbf{R}\underset{n}{\mathrm{lim}}\,\mathscr{K}_{n}=0
.
∎

2.
Constructible isocrystals

Constructible isocrystals on varieties were introduced in
[
LS16
]
, and their study continued in
[
LS17
,
LS22
]
, where most results are phrased in terms of Le Stum’s overconvergent site. It will be helpful to have a version of this formalism in the language of
realisations
of constructible isocrystals on tubes. In this section, the aim is therefore to restate many of the foundational results of
[
LS16
]
, but in the context of realisations.

For a frame
(X,Y,\mathfrak{P})
, I want to be able to work locally on
\mathfrak{P}
, which makes it important to work without properness hypotheses on
Y
. This makes it necessary to develop a theory of constructible isocrystals not just for varieties, but for pairs
(X,Y)
consisting of a variety
X
and a partial compactification
Y
. In fact, this case is implicitly dealt with in
[
LS16
]
, however, it will be more convenient here to phrase everything from the beginning in terms of realisations.

2.1.
Constructible modules

I start by introducing the
\mathcal{O}
-modules underlying constructible isocrystals.

2.1.1 Definition
.

Let
(X,Y,\mathfrak{P})
be a frame, and let
\mathscr{F}
be an
\mathcal{O}_{\left]X\right[_{\mathfrak{P}}}
-module. Then
\mathscr{F}
is called
constructible
if there exists a stratification
\{i_{\alpha}\colon X_{\alpha}\rightarrow X\}_{\alpha\in A}
such that, for each
\alpha\in A
,
i_{\alpha}^{-1}\mathscr{F}
is a coherent
\mathcal{O}_{\left]X_{\alpha}\right[}
-module. It is called
constructible locally free
if a stratification can be chosen such that each
i_{\alpha}^{-1}\mathscr{F}
is a locally finite free
\mathcal{O}_{\left]X_{\alpha}\right[_{\mathfrak{P}}}
-module.

The following facts about constructible and constructible locally free modules can be verified immediately.

(1)

If
\mathscr{F}
is an
\mathcal{O}_{\left]X\right[_{\mathfrak{P}}}
-module, and
\{i_{\alpha}\colon X_{\alpha}\rightarrow X\}_{\alpha\in A}
is a stratification of
X
, then
\mathscr{F}
is constructible (resp. constructible locally free) if and only if each
i_{\alpha}^{-1}\mathscr{F}
is.

(2)

If
i\colon Z\rightarrow X
is a locally closed immersion, and
\mathscr{F}
is an
\mathcal{O}_{\left]Z\right[_{\mathfrak{P}}}
-module, then
\mathscr{F}
is constructible (resp. constructible locally free) if and only if
i_{!}\mathscr{F}
is constructible (resp. constructible locally free) as an
\mathcal{O}_{\left]X\right[_{\mathfrak{P}}}
-module. The exact functors

i_{!}\colon{\bf Mod}(\mathcal{O}_{\left]Z\right[_{\mathfrak{P}}})\leftrightarrows{\bf Mod}(\mathcal{O}_{\left]X\right[_{\mathfrak{P}}})\colon i^{-1}

induce an equivalence of categories between constructible (resp. constructible locally free)
\mathcal{O}_{\left]Z\right[_{\mathfrak{P}}}
-modules, and constructible (resp. constructible locally free)
\mathcal{O}_{\left]X\right[_{\mathfrak{P}}}
-modules which are supported on
\left]Z\right[_{\mathfrak{P}}
.

(3)

If
i\colon Z\rightarrow X
is a closed immersion, with open complement
j\colon U\rightarrow X
, then every constructible (resp. constructible locally free)
\mathcal{O}_{\left]X\right[_{\mathfrak{P}}}
-module
\mathscr{F}
sits in a short exact sequence

0\rightarrow i_{!}i^{-1}\mathscr{F}\rightarrow\mathscr{F}\rightarrow j_{*}j^{-1}\mathscr{F}\rightarrow 0

where
i^{-1}\mathscr{F}
and
j^{-1}\mathscr{F}
are constructible (resp. constructible locally free) modules on
\left]Z\right[_{\mathfrak{P}}
and
\left]U\right[_{\mathfrak{P}}
respectively.

(4)

Every constructible (resp. constructible locally free)
\mathcal{O}_{\left]X\right[_{\mathfrak{P}}}
-module
\mathscr{F}
admits a finite composition series

0=\mathscr{F}_{0}\subset\mathscr{F}_{1}\subset\ldots\subset\mathscr{F}_{n}=\mathscr{F},

such that, for each
1\leq\alpha\leq n
, there exists a locally closed subscheme
i_{\alpha}\colon Z_{\alpha}\rightarrow X
, a coherent (resp. locally finite free)
\mathcal{O}_{\left]Z_{\alpha}\right[_{\mathfrak{P}}}
-module
\mathscr{G}_{\alpha}
, and an isomorphism

\mathscr{F}_{\alpha}/\mathscr{F}_{\alpha-1}\overset{\cong}{\longrightarrow}i_{\alpha!}\mathscr{G}_{\alpha}.

(5)

The category of constructible (resp. constructible locally free)
\mathcal{O}_{\left]X\right[_{\mathfrak{P}}}
-module is an abelian subcategory of
{\bf Mod}(\mathcal{O}_{\left]X\right[_{\mathfrak{P}}})
, closed under extensions. In particular, every
\mathcal{O}_{\left]X\right[_{\mathfrak{P}}}
-module admitting a filtration as in (
4
) is constructible (resp. constructible locally free if each
\mathscr{G}_{\alpha}
is locally free).

(6)

If
\mathscr{F}
and
\mathscr{G}
are constructible (resp. constructible locally free) then so is
\mathscr{F}\otimes_{\mathcal{O}_{\left]X\right[_{\mathfrak{P}}}}\mathscr{G}
.

2.1.2 Remark
.

Note that the functors
i_{!}i^{-1}
and
j_{*}j^{-1}
appearing in (
3
) are the functors
\underline{\Gamma}^{\dagger}_{Z}
and
j_{U}^{\dagger}
introduced in §
1.2
above.

2.1.3 Lemma
.

Any constructible locally free
\mathcal{O}_{\left]X\right[_{\mathfrak{P}}}
-module is flat.

Proof.

Let
\{i_{\alpha}\colon X_{\alpha}\rightarrow X\}_{\alpha\in A}
be a stratification such that each
i_{\alpha}^{-1}\mathscr{F}
is locally finite free. Note that the tubes
\left]X_{\alpha}\right[_{\mathfrak{P}}
cover
\left]X\right[_{\mathfrak{P}}
, and, by definition, we have
\mathcal{O}_{\left]X_{\alpha}\right[_{\mathfrak{P}}}=i_{\alpha}^{-1}\mathcal{O}_{\left]X\right[_{\mathfrak{P}}}
. Since flatness can be checked on stalks, we can therefore replace
X
by
X_{\alpha}
, and thus assume that
\mathscr{F}
is a locally finite free
\mathcal{O}_{\left]X\right[_{\mathfrak{P}}}
-module. In this case the claim is clear.
∎

2.1.4 Remark
.

In general, constructible
\mathcal{O}_{\left]X\right[_{\mathfrak{P}}}
-modules which are not constructible locally free need not be flat.

2.1.5 Lemma
.

Suppose that
\mathscr{F}\in\mathbf{Mod}(\mathcal{O}_{\left]X\right[_{\mathfrak{P}}})
.

(1)

Let
\{\mathfrak{P}_{\alpha}\}_{\alpha\in A}
be a finite open cover of
\mathfrak{P}
, and
X_{\alpha}:=X\cap\mathfrak{P}_{\alpha}
. Then
\mathscr{F}
is constructible (resp. constructible locally free) if and only if each
\mathscr{F}|_{\left]X_{\alpha}\right[_{\mathfrak{P}_{\alpha}}}
is.

(2)

Let
\{X_{\alpha}\}_{\alpha\in A}
be a finite open cover of
X
. Then
\mathscr{F}
is constructible (resp. constructible locally free) if and only if each
\mathscr{F}|_{\left]X_{\alpha}\right[_{\mathfrak{P}}}
is.

Proof.

In both cases the ‘only if’ direction is clear, I will prove the ‘if’ direction.

(1)

Given stratifications of each
X_{\alpha}
, there exists a stratification of
X
whose restriction to each
X_{\alpha}
is a refinement of the given one of
X
. Working on each of the given strata, the question therefore amounts to showing that if
\mathscr{F}
is an
\mathcal{O}_{\left]X\right[_{\mathfrak{P}}}
-module, and each
\mathscr{F}|_{\left]X_{\alpha}\right[_{\mathfrak{P}_{\alpha}}}
is coherent (resp. locally finite free), then
\mathscr{F}
is coherent (resp. locally finite free). In this case, the tubes
\left]X_{\alpha}\right[_{\mathfrak{P}_{\alpha}}=\left]X\right[_{\mathfrak{P}}\cap\mathfrak{P}_{\alpha K}
form an open cover of
\left]X\right[_{\mathfrak{P}}
, and the claim then follows.

(2)

Arguing similarly, I need to show that if
\mathscr{F}
is an
\mathcal{O}_{\left]X\right[_{\mathfrak{P}}}
-module, and each
\mathscr{F}|_{\left]X_{\alpha}\right[_{\mathfrak{P}}}
is coherent (resp. locally finite free), then
\mathscr{F}
is coherent (resp. locally finite free). Thanks to
[
Ber96a
, Proposition 2.1.8]
,
\mathscr{F}
sits in an exact sequence

0\rightarrow\mathscr{F}\rightarrow\bigoplus_{\alpha\in A}j_{X_{\alpha}}^{\dagger}\mathscr{F}\rightarrow\bigoplus_{\alpha,\beta}j_{X_{\alpha}\cap X_{\beta}}^{\dagger}\mathscr{F},

and applying
[
LS07
, Proposition 5.4.12]
shows that the unique such
\mathcal{O}_{\left]X\right[_{\mathfrak{P}}}
-module
\mathscr{F}
has to be coherent. If each
j_{X_{\alpha}}^{\dagger}\mathscr{F}
is moreover locally free, then following through the
proof
of
[
LS07
, Proposition 5.4.12]
, which is essentially based upon Proposition
1.3.3
quoted above, proves that in fact
\mathscr{F}
is locally free. ∎

If
u\colon(X^{\prime},Y^{\prime},\mathfrak{P}^{\prime})\rightarrow(X,Y,\mathfrak{P})
is a morphism of frames, it is a straightforward check to show that the functor

u^{*}\colon{\bf Mod}(\mathcal{O}_{\left]X\right[_{\mathfrak{P}}})\rightarrow{\bf Mod}(\mathcal{O}_{\left]X^{\prime}\right[_{\mathfrak{P}^{\prime}}})

preserves both constructible and constructible locally free modules. I don’t know in what generality, the projection formula holds for constructible
\mathcal{O}_{\left]X\right[_{\mathfrak{P}}}
-modules, but the following special cases will suffice.

2.1.6 Proposition
.

Let
\mathfrak{P}
be a flat formal scheme,
X\hookrightarrow\mathfrak{P}
a locally closed immersion,
\mathscr{F}
a constructible locally free
\mathcal{O}_{\left]X\right[_{\mathfrak{P}}}
-module, and
f\colon V\rightarrow\left]X\right[_{\mathfrak{P}}
a morphism of germs.

(1)

If
f
is quasi-compact, then the map

{\bf R}f_{*}\mathcal{O}_{V}\otimes^{{\bf L}}_{\mathcal{O}_{\left]X\right[_{\mathfrak{P}}}}\mathscr{F}\rightarrow{\bf R}f_{*}{\bf L}f^{*}\mathscr{F}

is an isomorphism in
{\bf D}(\mathcal{O}_{\left]X\right[_{\mathfrak{P}}})
.

(2)

If
f
is partially proper, then there is a natural (in
\mathscr{F}
) isomorphism

{\bf R}f_{!}\mathcal{O}_{V}\otimes^{{\bf L}}_{\mathcal{O}_{\left]X\right[_{\mathfrak{P}}}}\mathscr{F}\overset{\cong}{\longrightarrow}{\bf R}f_{!}{\bf L}f^{*}\mathscr{F}

in
{\bf D}^{+}(\mathcal{O}_{\left]X\right[_{\mathfrak{P}}})
.

Proof.

The proofs in the two cases both ultimately rely on the same result in general topology: if
g\colon S\rightarrow T
is a coherent
12
12

12
