quasi-compact and quasi-separated
morphism of locally coherent
13
13

13

coherent = quasi-compact, quasi-separated, and with a basis of quasi-compact opens
and sober
14
14

14

every irreducible subset has a unique generic point
topological spaces,
\mathscr{G}
is a sheaf on
S
,
t\in T
, and
G(t)\subset T
is the set of generalisations of
t
, then

({\bf R}^{q}g_{*}\mathscr{G})_{t}\overset{\cong}{\longrightarrow}{\rm H}^{q}(g^{-1}(G(t)),\mathscr{G})

for all
q\geq 0
. This can be seen, for example, by taking a cofinal system
\{U_{i}\}_{i\in I}
of neighbourhoods of
t
in
T
, and applying
[
FK18
, Chapter 0, Proposition 3.1.19]
to the inverse system
g^{-1}(U_{i})
. It then follows, by passing to stalks, that if
i\colon T^{\prime}\rightarrow T
is the inclusion of a locally closed subset which is stable under generalisation, then the base change map

i^{-1}{\bf R}g_{*}\rightarrow{\bf R}g^{\prime}_{*}i^{\prime-1}

associated to the Cartesian square

\textstyle{S^{\prime}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{i^{\prime}}
\scriptstyle{g^{\prime}}
\textstyle{S\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{g}
\textstyle{T^{\prime}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{i}
\textstyle{T}

is an isomorphism.

In case (
1
), the dependence on this base change result is explicit in the proof. In this case, since constructible locally free modules are flat, the tensor products and pullbacks in the statement above are really underived, thus I need to show that the map

{\bf R}f_{*}\mathcal{O}_{V}\otimes_{\mathcal{O}_{\left]X\right[_{\mathfrak{P}}}}\mathscr{F}\rightarrow{\bf R}f_{*}f^{*}\mathscr{F}

is an isomorphism. Choose a stratification
\left\{i_{\alpha}\colon X_{\alpha}\rightarrow X\right\}_{\alpha\in A}
such that each
\mathscr{F}\!\mid_{\left]X_{\alpha}\right[_{\mathfrak{P}}}
is locally finite free. Letting

f_{\alpha}\colon V_{\alpha}:=f^{-1}\left(\left]X_{\alpha}\right[_{\mathfrak{P}}\right)\rightarrow\left]X_{\alpha}\right[_{\mathfrak{P}}

denote the pullback, the classical projection formula shows that

{\bf R}f_{\alpha*}\mathcal{O}_{V_{\alpha}}\otimes_{\mathcal{O}_{\left]X_{\alpha}\right[_{\mathfrak{P}}}}(\mathscr{F}\!\mid_{\left]X_{\alpha}\right[_{\mathfrak{P}}})\rightarrow{\bf R}f_{\alpha*}f_{\alpha}^{*}(\mathscr{F}\!\mid_{\left]X_{\alpha}\right[_{\mathfrak{P}}})

is an isomorphism. Hence, arguing via stalks, it suffices to show that for each
\alpha\in A
, the base change map

({\bf R}f_{*}f^{*}\mathscr{F})\!\mid_{\left]X_{\alpha}\right[_{\mathfrak{P}}}\rightarrow{\bf R}f_{\alpha*}f_{\alpha}^{*}(\mathscr{F}\!\mid_{\left]X_{\alpha}\right[_{\mathfrak{P}}})

is an isomorphism, together with the analogous result with
\mathscr{F}
replaced by
\mathcal{O}_{\left]X\right[_{\mathfrak{P}}}
. Since each
\left]X_{\alpha}\right[_{\mathfrak{P}}\subset\left]X\right[_{\mathfrak{P}}
is stable under generalisation, this follows from the general topological result above.

In case (
2
), the dependence on the above-mentioned ‘base change’ result is hidden in the proof of Proposition
1.4.7
. In this case, I first construct a morphism

(2.1.1)

\mathscr{F}\otimes_{\mathcal{O}_{\left]X\right[_{\mathfrak{P}}}}\mathbf{R}f_{!}\mathcal{O}_{V}\rightarrow\mathbf{R}f_{!}f^{*}\mathscr{F}.

To do this, choose an injective resolution
\mathcal{O}_{V}\rightarrow\mathscr{I}
. Then flatness of
\mathscr{F}
over
\mathcal{O}_{\left]X\right[_{\mathfrak{P}}}
implies that
f^{*}\mathscr{F}\rightarrow f^{*}\mathscr{F}\otimes_{\mathcal{O}_{V}}\mathscr{I}
is a quasi-isomorphism. Next, choose an injective resolution
f^{*}\mathscr{F}\otimes_{\mathcal{O}_{V}}\mathscr{I}\rightarrow\mathscr{J}
, so that
\mathscr{J}
is also an injective resolution of
f^{*}\mathscr{F}
. It is therefore enough to construct a map

\mathscr{F}\otimes_{\mathcal{O}_{\left]X\right[_{\mathfrak{P}}}}f_{!}\mathscr{I}\rightarrow f_{!}\mathscr{J}.

But now, such a map can be found factoring through the map

f_{!}(f^{*}\mathscr{F}\otimes_{\mathcal{O}_{V}}\mathscr{I})\rightarrow f_{!}\mathscr{J}

simply by noting that the map

\mathscr{F}\otimes_{\mathcal{O}_{\left]X\right[_{\mathfrak{P}}}}f_{*}\mathscr{I}\rightarrow f_{*}(f^{*}\mathscr{F}\otimes_{\mathcal{O}_{V}}\mathscr{I})

coming from adjunction sends

\mathscr{F}\otimes_{\mathcal{O}_{\left]X\right[_{\mathfrak{P}}}}f_{!}\mathscr{I}\subset\mathscr{F}\otimes_{\mathcal{O}_{\left]X\right[_{\mathfrak{P}}}}f_{*}\mathscr{I}

into

f_{!}(f^{*}\mathscr{F}\otimes_{\mathcal{O}_{V}}\mathscr{I})\subset f_{*}(f^{*}\mathscr{F}\otimes_{\mathcal{O}_{V}}\mathscr{I}).

The proof that (
2.1.1
) is an isomorphism now proceeds exactly as in case (
1
), using dévissage and Proposition
1.4.7
to reduce the projection formula for locally free sheaves, that is, to Lemma
1.4.6
.
∎

2.2.
Convergent stratifications and connections

I now introduction convergent stratifications on
\mathcal{O}_{\left]X\right[_{\mathfrak{P}}}
-modules. If
(X,Y,\mathfrak{P})
is a frame, then embedding
Y
in
\mathfrak{P}^{2}
via the diagonal gives rise to a frame
(X,Y,\mathfrak{P}^{2})
. Consider the two morphisms of frames

p_{0},p_{1}\colon(X,Y,\mathfrak{P}^{2})\rightarrow(X,Y,\mathfrak{P})

coming from the projection maps
p_{i}\colon\mathfrak{P}^{2}\rightarrow\mathfrak{P}
, as well as the morphism

\Delta\colon(X,Y,\mathfrak{P})\rightarrow(X,Y,\mathfrak{P}^{2})

induced by the diagonal map
\Delta\colon\mathfrak{P}\rightarrow\mathfrak{P}^{2}
, which is a common section to the
p_{i}
. As in Remark
1.2.5
, I will write

p_{i}\colon\left]X\right[_{\mathfrak{P}^{2}}\rightarrow\left]X\right[_{\mathfrak{P}},\;\;\;\;\Delta\colon\left]X\right[_{\mathfrak{P}}\rightarrow\left]X\right[_{\mathfrak{P}^{2}}

for the induced morphisms of tubes.

2.2.1 Definition
.

Let
(X,Y,\mathfrak{P})
be a frame with
\mathfrak{P}
smooth around
X
, and
\mathscr{F}
an
\mathcal{O}_{\left]X\right[_{\mathfrak{P}}}
-module. Then a convergent stratification on
\mathscr{F}
is an isomorphism

\epsilon\colon p_{1}^{*}\mathscr{F}\rightarrow p_{0}^{*}\mathscr{F}

of
\mathcal{O}_{\left]X\right[_{\mathfrak{P}^{2}}}
-modules, restricting to the identity along
\Delta
, and satisfying the cocycle condition on
\left]X\right[_{\mathfrak{P}^{3}}
.

2.2.2 Remark
.

(1)

Of course, the definition makes sense without any smoothness hypothesis on
\mathfrak{P}
, but will only be a reasonable one when
\mathfrak{P}
is smooth around
X
.

(2)

Note that what I have called ‘convergent’ here might more properly be termed ‘overconvergent along
Y\setminus X
’. Given the wide range of different kinds of isocrystals that will appear in this article, I have chosen the slightly simpler terminology to avoid multiplying adjectives beyond necessity. I will try to avoid any potential confusion this might cause.

Now let
\mathfrak{P}^{(n)}
denote the
n
th infinitesimal neighbourhood of
\mathfrak{P}
inside
\mathfrak{P}^{2}
. The restriction of
\epsilon
to each
\left]X\right[_{\mathfrak{P}^{(n)}}
gives rise to a stratification on
\mathscr{F}
in the usual sense. Since
\mathfrak{P}
is smooth around
X
, there exists an open neighbourhood
\left]X\right[_{\mathfrak{P}}\subset V\subset\left]Y\right[_{\mathfrak{P}}
which is smooth, and hence any such stratification can be viewed as an integrable connection. This gives rise to a faithful (but not in general full) functor from modules with convergent stratification on
\left]X\right[_{\mathfrak{P}}
to modules with integrable connection on
\left]X\right[_{\mathfrak{P}}
.

2.2.3 Definition
.

A constructible isocrystal on
(X,Y,\mathfrak{P})
is a constructible
\mathcal{O}_{\left]X\right[_{\mathfrak{P}}}
-module
\mathscr{F}
together with a convergent stratification
\epsilon
on
\mathscr{F}
. The category of constructible isocrystals on
(X,Y,\mathfrak{P})
is denoted
\mathbf{Isoc}_{\mathrm{cons}}(X,Y,\mathfrak{P})
.

In the special case that
X=Y=P
(and therefore
\mathfrak{P}
is everywhere smooth) I will generally write
\mathbf{Isoc}_{\mathrm{cons}}(\mathfrak{P})
instead of
\mathbf{Isoc}_{\mathrm{cons}}(P,P,\mathfrak{P})
, and call these objects constructible isocrystals on
\mathfrak{P}
.

2.2.4 Lemma
.

The
\mathcal{O}_{\left]X\right[_{\mathfrak{P}}}
-module underlying any
\mathscr{F}\in\mathbf{Isoc}_{\mathrm{cons}}(X,Y,\mathfrak{P})
is constructible locally free, and in particular, flat.

Proof.

Since
\mathfrak{P}
is smooth around
X
, it is also smooth around any locally closed subscheme of
X
. After possibly passing to a suitable stratification of
X
, I may assume that
\mathscr{F}
itself is coherent.

Applying Proposition
1.3.3
I can then choose an open neighbourhood
V
of
\left]X\right[_{\mathfrak{P}}
such that
\mathscr{F}
extends to a coherent
\mathcal{O}_{V}
-module. Since
\left]X\right[_{\mathfrak{P}^{(n)}}
has the same underlying topological spaces as
\left]X\right[_{\mathfrak{P}}
, applying Proposition
1.3.3
on
\left]X\right[_{\mathfrak{P}^{(n)}}
shows that, after possibly shrinking
V
further, the stratification (and hence the integrable connection) on
\mathscr{F}
also extends to
V
.

It therefore suffices to show that coherent
\mathcal{O}_{V}
-modules admitting integrable connections are locally finite free. Since
V
is smooth over
K
, this is well-known, see for example
[
Ked06
, Lemma 3.3.4]
.
∎

Inside
\mathbf{Isoc}_{\mathrm{cons}}(X,Y,\mathfrak{P})
there is a natural full subcategory consisting of objects whose underlying
\mathcal{O}_{\left]X\right[_{\mathfrak{P}}}
-module is coherent, or equivalently locally free. I will denote this subcategory by

\mathbf{Isoc}(X,Y,\mathfrak{P})\subset\mathbf{Isoc}_{\mathrm{cons}}(X,Y,\mathfrak{P})

and refer to its objects as locally free isocrystals on
(X,Y,\mathfrak{P})
. Again, these objects have traditionally been called ‘partially overconvergent isocrystals’, and I will try to avoid any confusion that may arise from the more simplistic terminology I’ve chosen to use here.

2.2.5 Proposition
.

The forgetful functor from
\mathbf{Isoc}_{\mathrm{cons}}(X,Y,\mathfrak{P})
to
\mathcal{O}_{\left]X\right[_{\mathfrak{P}}}
-modules with integrable connection is fully faithful.

Proof.

This is
[
LS16
, Proposition 4.8]
.
∎

Thus
\mathbf{Isoc}_{\mathrm{cons}}(X,Y,\mathfrak{P})
can be viewed as a full subcategory of
\mathbf{Mod}(\mathscr{D}_{\left]X\right[_{\mathfrak{P}}})
. It is in fact an abelian subcategory by
[
LS16
, Proposition 4.3]
. It also follows relatively quickly from the definitions that if
\mathscr{F}
and
\mathscr{G}
are constructible isocrystals, then
\mathscr{F}\otimes_{\mathcal{O}_{\left]X\right[_{\mathfrak{P}}}}\mathscr{G}
can be endowed with the structure of a constructible isocrystal, by Proposition
2.2.5
this is uniquely determined by the fact that this is compatible (via the natural forgetful functor) with the tensor product (over
\mathcal{O}_{\left]X\right[_{\mathfrak{P}}}
) of
\mathscr{D}_{\left]X\right[_{\mathfrak{P}}}
-modules. Similarly, if

\textstyle{X^{\prime}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{Y^{\prime}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{\mathfrak{P}^{\prime}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{X\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{Y\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{\mathfrak{P}}

is a morphism of frames, with
\mathfrak{P}^{\prime}
smooth around
X^{\prime}
and
\mathfrak{P}
smooth around
X
, then the pullback functor

\left]f\right[_{u}^{*}\colon\mathbf{Mod}(\mathscr{D}_{\left]X\right[_{\mathfrak{P}}})\rightarrow\mathbf{Mod}(\mathscr{D}_{\left]X^{\prime}\right[_{\mathfrak{P}^{\prime}}})

sends
\mathbf{Isoc}_{\mathrm{cons}}(X,Y,\mathfrak{P})
into
\mathbf{Isoc}_{\mathrm{cons}}(X^{\prime},Y^{\prime},\mathfrak{P}^{\prime})
, essentially because
\left]f\right[_{u}^{*}
commutes with the maps
p_{i}^{*}
used to construct convergent stratifications. Of course, pullback commutes with tensor product in the obvious sense.

The analogues for constructible isocrystals of each of the ‘elementary facts’ about constructible modules are true, but some of them are rather harder to prove. We start with the following.

2.2.6 Lemma
.

Let
i\colon Z\rightarrow X
be a locally closed immersion, and
\overline{Z}
the closure of
Z
in
P
. Then the exact functors

i_{!}\colon\mathbf{Mod}(\mathscr{D}_{\left]Z\right[_{\mathfrak{P}}})\leftrightarrows\mathbf{Mod}(\mathscr{D}_{\left]X\right[_{\mathfrak{P}}})\colon i^{-1}

preserve constructible isocrystals, and induce an equivalence of categories between
\mathbf{Isoc}_{\mathrm{cons}}(Z,\overline{Z},\mathfrak{P})
and the full subcategory of
\mathbf{Isoc}_{\mathrm{cons}}(X,Y,\mathfrak{P})
consisting of objects supported on
\left]Z\right[_{\mathfrak{P}}
.

2.2.7 Remark
.

In this situation, I will generally refer to constructible isocrystals being supported on
Z
, rather than being supported on
\left]Z\right[_{\mathfrak{P}}
.

Proof.

The fact that each of
i^{-1}
and
i_{!}
preserve constructible isocrystals follows from the fact that each commutes with
p_{i}^{*}
. For
i^{-1}
this is pretty much immediate from the definitions, for
i_{!}
this is the content of Lemma
1.2.6
. The statement on the equivalence of categories then follows.
∎

2.2.8 Corollary
.

Let
i\colon Z\rightarrow X
be a closed immersion, with open complement
j\colon U\rightarrow X
, and let
\overline{Z}
be the closure of
Z
inside
P
. Then every constructible isocrystal
\mathscr{F}\in\mathbf{Isoc}_{\mathrm{cons}}(X,Y,\mathfrak{P})
sits in a short exact sequence

0\rightarrow i_{!}i^{-1}\mathscr{F}\rightarrow\mathscr{F}\rightarrow j_{*}j^{-1}\mathscr{F}\rightarrow 0

where
i^{-1}\mathscr{F}\in\mathbf{Isoc}_{\mathrm{cons}}(Z,\overline{Z},\mathfrak{P})
and
j^{-1}\mathscr{F}\in\mathbf{Isoc}_{\mathrm{cons}}(U,Y,\mathfrak{P})
.

2.2.9 Corollary
.

Let
\mathscr{F}\in\mathbf{Isoc}_{\mathrm{cons}}(X,Y,\mathfrak{P})
. Then
\mathscr{F}
admits a finite composition series

0=\mathscr{F}_{0}\subset\mathscr{F}_{1}\subset\ldots\subset\mathscr{F}_{n}=\mathscr{F},

such that for each
1\leq\alpha\leq n
, there exists a locally closed subscheme
i_{\alpha}\colon X_{\alpha}\rightarrow X
, with closure
\overline{X}_{\alpha}
in
P
, a locally free isocrystal
\mathscr{G}_{\alpha}\in\mathbf{Isoc}(X_{\alpha},\overline{X}_{\alpha},\mathfrak{P})
, and an isomorphism

\mathscr{F}_{\alpha}/\mathscr{F}_{\alpha-1}\overset{\cong}{\longrightarrow}i_{\alpha!}\mathscr{G}_{\alpha}.

2.2.10 Remark
.

I will call objects of the form
i_{\alpha!}\mathscr{G}_{\alpha}
‘locally free isocrystals supported on
X_{\alpha}
’. Thus a more succinct expression of the corollary is that every constructible isocrystal on
X
is an iterated extension of locally free isocrystals supported on locally closed subschemes of
X
.

2.2.11 Lemma
.

Suppose that
\mathscr{F}\in\mathbf{Mod}(\mathscr{D}_{\left]X\right[_{\mathfrak{P}}})
.

(1)

Let
\{\mathfrak{P}_{\alpha}\}_{\alpha\in A}
be a finite open cover of
\mathfrak{P}
, and set
X_{\alpha}=X\cap\mathfrak{P}_{\alpha}
. Then
\mathscr{F}
is a constructible isocrystal if and only if each
\mathscr{F}|_{\left]X_{\alpha}\right[_{\mathfrak{P}_{\alpha}}}
is.

(2)

Let
\{X_{\alpha}\}_{\alpha\in A}
be a finite open cover of
X
. Then
\mathscr{F}
is a constructible isocrystal if and only if each
\mathscr{F}|_{\left]X_{\alpha}\right[_{\mathfrak{P}}}
is.

Proof.

As in the proof of Lemma
2.1.5
, in both cases the ‘only if’ direction is clear, and I will concentrate on the ‘if’ direction. In both cases, the constructibility was proved in
2.1.5
, so what is left to prove is the convergence of the stratification. The case of an open cover of
\mathfrak{P}
is straightforward, since this induces an open cover of
\left]X\right[_{\mathfrak{P}^{2}}
over which the convergent stratification
\epsilon
can be constructed (that they coincide on the intersections follows from the full faithfulness result Proposition
2.2.5
).

For the case of an open cover of
X
, I argue as in Lemma
2.1.5
, and write
\mathscr{F}
as the kernel of the map

(2.2.1)

\bigoplus_{\alpha}j_{X_{\alpha}}^{\dagger}\mathscr{F}\rightarrow\bigoplus_{\alpha,\beta}j^{\dagger}_{X_{\alpha}\cap X_{\beta}}\mathscr{F}.

The fact that each
\mathscr{F}|_{\left]X_{\alpha}\right[_{\mathfrak{P}}}
is a constructible isocrystal, together with Lemma
2.2.6
, implies that both terms in (
2.2.1
) are constructible isocrystals. Hence I can conclude using the fact that
\mathbf{Isoc}_{\mathrm{cons}}(X,Y,\mathfrak{P})
is an abelian subcategory of
\mathbf{Mod}(\mathscr{D}_{\left]X\right[_{\mathfrak{P}}})
.
∎

The final two properties to establish are the facts that constructible isocrystals can be detected over a stratification of
X
, and are stable under extensions. The proof of the latter of these depends on the former, which is a lot involved than the properties we have established so far, and is in fact the first result in this section that genuinely goes beyond what was proved in
[
LS16
]
. The key calculation that we need in the proof (this calculation is Lemma
2.2.13
below) applies in the following situation.

2.2.12 Setup
.

Suppose that
(X,Y,\mathfrak{P})
is a frame with
\mathfrak{P}
rig-smooth around
X
, and consider the morphism of frames

\textstyle{\widehat{{\mathbb{A}}}^{d}_{\mathfrak{P}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\pi}
\textstyle{X\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{Y\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{\mathfrak{P}}

where
Y
is embedded into
\widehat{{\mathbb{A}}}^{d}_{\mathfrak{P}}
via the zero section, and
\pi
is the projection. Let
\mathfrak{O}^{(n)}_{\mathfrak{P}}\subset\widehat{{\mathbb{A}}}^{d}_{\mathfrak{P}}
denote the
n
th infinitesimal neighbourhood of the zero section, and write
\pi_{n}:\mathfrak{O}^{(n)}_{\mathfrak{P}}\rightarrow\mathfrak{P}
for the projection. Let

i\colon Z\rightarrow X\leftarrow U\colon j

be a complementary closed and open immersion. If
T=X,U
or
Z
we write

\displaystyle\pi

\displaystyle\colon\left]T\right[_{\widehat{{\mathbb{A}}}^{d}_{\mathfrak{P}}}\rightarrow\left]T\right[_{\mathfrak{P}}

\displaystyle\pi_{n}

\displaystyle\colon\left]T\right[_{\mathfrak{O}_{\mathfrak{P}}^{(n)}}\rightarrow\left]T\right[_{\mathfrak{P}}

for the projections. Similarly, if
\mathfrak{T}=\mathfrak{P}
,
\widehat{{\mathbb{A}}}^{d}_{\mathfrak{P}}
or
\mathfrak{O}^{(n)}_{\mathfrak{P}}
we write

i\colon\left]Z\right[_{\mathfrak{T}}\rightarrow\left]X\right[_{\mathfrak{T}}\leftarrow\left]U\right[_{\mathfrak{T}}\colon j

for the immersions.

2.2.13 Lemma
.

Consider Setup
2.2.12
, and assume that, locally on
Y
,
U\subset Y
is the complement of a hypersurface. Let
\mathscr{F}
be a coherent
\mathcal{O}_{\left]U\right[_{\widehat{{\mathbb{A}}}^{d}_{\mathfrak{P}}}}
-module, and
\mathscr{G}
a constructible locally free
\mathcal{O}_{\left]Z\right[_{\mathfrak{P}}}
-module. Then the natural map

{\rm Hom}_{\mathcal{O}_{\left]U\right[_{\widehat{{\mathbb{A}}}^{d}_{\mathfrak{P}}}}}(\mathscr{F},j^{-1}i_{*}\pi^{*}\mathscr{G})\rightarrow\underset{n}{\mathrm{lim}}\,{\rm Hom}_{\mathcal{O}_{\left]U\right[_{\mathfrak{O}^{(n)}_{\mathfrak{P}}}}}(\mathscr{F},j^{-1}i_{*}\pi^{*}\mathscr{G})

is injective.

Proof.

The question is local on
\mathfrak{P}
(and hence
Y
), so we may assume that
\mathfrak{P}
is affine, and that
U\subset Y
is the complement of a hypersurface. The functors
{\rm Hom}(\mathscr{F},-)
,
j^{-1}
,
i_{*}
,
\pi^{*}
,
\underset{n}{\mathrm{lim}}\,
,
\pi_{n}^{*}
are all left exact, and so the claim is additive over short exact sequences in
\mathscr{G}
. I can therefore assume that there exists a locally closed immersion
a\colon T\rightarrow Z
and a locally free
\mathcal{O}_{\left]T\right[_{\mathfrak{P}}}
-module
\mathscr{H}
such that
\mathscr{G}=a_{!}\mathscr{H}
. I will continue to use
\pi
and
\pi_{n}
to denote the natural maps

\displaystyle\pi

\displaystyle\colon\left]T\right[_{\widehat{{\mathbb{A}}}^{d}_{\mathfrak{P}}}\rightarrow\left]T\right[_{\mathfrak{P}}

\displaystyle\pi_{n}

\displaystyle\colon\left]T\right[_{\mathfrak{O}^{(n)}_{\mathfrak{P}}}\rightarrow\left]T\right[_{\mathfrak{P}}.

Since
a_{!}
commutes with
\pi^{*}
by Lemma
1.2.6
, the natural map
a_{!}\rightarrow a_{*}
is injective, and
i_{*}
,
j^{-1}
are left exact, there is a natural injective map

j^{-1}i_{*}\pi^{*}\mathscr{G}=j^{-1}i_{*}\pi^{*}a_{!}\mathscr{H}=j^{-1}i_{*}a_{!}\pi^{*}\mathscr{H}\rightarrow j^{-1}i_{*}a_{*}\pi^{*}\mathscr{H}.

Similarly, there is an injective map

j^{-1}i_{*}\pi_{n}^{*}\mathscr{G}\rightarrow j^{-1}i_{*}a_{*}\pi_{n}^{*}\mathscr{H}.

Again appealing to left exactness of
{\rm Hom}
and
{\rm lim}
, it suffices to show that the map

(2.2.2)

{\rm Hom}_{\mathcal{O}_{\left]U\right[_{\widehat{{\mathbb{A}}}^{d}_{\mathfrak{P}}}}}(\mathscr{F},j^{-1}i_{*}a_{*}\pi^{*}\mathscr{H})\rightarrow\underset{n}{\mathrm{lim}}\,{\rm Hom}_{\mathcal{O}_{\left]U\right[_{\mathfrak{O}^{(n)}_{\mathfrak{P}}}}}(\mathscr{F},j^{-1}i_{*}a_{*}\pi_{n}^{*}\mathscr{H})

is injective. Next, let
i^{\prime}\colon Z\rightarrow Y
and
j^{\prime}:U\rightarrow Y
denote the inclusions into
Y
, thus

j^{\prime-1}i^{\prime}_{*}=j^{-1}i_{*}.

This shows that I can replace
j^{-1}i_{*}a_{*}\pi^{*}\mathscr{H}
with
j^{\prime-1}i^{\prime}_{*}a_{*}\pi^{*}\mathscr{H}
, and similarly
j^{-1}i_{*}a_{*}\pi_{n}^{*}\mathscr{H}
with
j^{\prime-1}i^{\prime}_{*}a_{*}\pi_{n}^{*}\mathscr{H}
.

Now, the given claim is local on the tube
\left]Y\right[_{\mathfrak{P}}
of
Y
, so replacing
\mathfrak{P}
by a formal model for the closed tubes
[Y]_{\mathfrak{P}\eta}
for varying
\eta
, I can assume that
Y=P
. The LHS of (
2.2.2
) is now identified with

{\rm Hom}_{\mathcal{O}_{{\mathbb{D}}^{d}_{K}(0;1^{-})\times_{K}\left]U\right[_{\mathfrak{P}}}}(\mathscr{F},j^{\prime-1}i^{\prime}_{*}a_{*}\pi^{*}\mathscr{H})=\underset{\rho<1}{\mathrm{lim}}\,{\rm Hom}_{\mathcal{O}_{{\mathbb{D}}^{d}_{K}(0;\rho)\times_{K}\left]U\right[_{\mathfrak{P}}}}(\mathscr{F},j^{\prime-1}i^{\prime}_{*}a_{*}\pi^{*}\mathscr{H}),

so it is enough to show that the natural map

{\rm Hom}_{\mathcal{O}_{{\mathbb{D}}^{d}_{K}(0;\rho)\times_{K}\left]U\right[_{\mathfrak{P}}}}(\mathscr{F},j^{\prime-1}i^{\prime}_{*}a_{*}\pi^{*}\mathscr{H})\rightarrow\underset{n}{\mathrm{lim}}\,{\rm Hom}_{\mathcal{O}_{\left]U\right[_{\mathfrak{O}^{(n)}_{\mathfrak{P}}}}}(\mathscr{F},j^{-1}i_{*}a_{*}\pi_{n}^{*}\mathscr{H})

is injective, for each
\rho<1
.

Let
\{V_{\lambda}\}_{\lambda\geq\lambda_{0}}
be a cofinal sequence of neighbourhoods of
\left]U\right[_{\mathfrak{P}}
in
\left]Y\right[_{\mathfrak{P}}=\mathfrak{P}_{K}
. Since
\mathfrak{P}
is affine, and
U
is the complement of a hypersurface in
Y=P
, we can take each
V_{\lambda}
to be affinoid.

By quasi-compactness,
{\mathbb{D}}^{d}_{K}(0;\rho)\times_{K}V_{\lambda}
(resp.
\mathfrak{O}^{(n)}_{\mathfrak{P},K}\times_{\mathfrak{P}_{K}}V_{\lambda}
) is a cofinal system of neighbourhoods of
{\mathbb{D}}^{d}_{K}(0;\rho)\times_{K}\left]U\right[_{\mathfrak{P}}
(resp.
\mathfrak{O}^{(n)}_{\mathfrak{P},K}\times_{\mathfrak{P}_{K}}\left]U\right[_{\mathfrak{P}}
) inside
{\mathbb{D}}^{d}_{K}(0;\rho)\times_{K}\mathfrak{P}_{K}
(resp.
\mathfrak{O}^{(n)}_{\mathfrak{P},K}
). Thus, for all sufficiently large
\lambda
,
\mathscr{F}
extends to a coherent sheaf on
{\mathbb{D}}^{d}_{K}(0;\rho)\times_{K}V_{\lambda}
by Proposition
1.3.3
. Since
{\mathbb{D}}^{d}_{K}(0;\rho)\times_{K}V_{\lambda}
is affinoid, the extension of
\mathscr{F}
to
{\mathbb{D}}^{d}_{K}(0;\rho)\times_{K}V_{\lambda}
is generated by finitely many global section. Hence
\mathscr{F}
is generated by finitely many global sections as a coherent
\mathcal{O}_{{\mathbb{D}}^{d}_{K}(0;\rho)\times_{K}\left]U\right[_{\mathfrak{P}}}
-module. Thus choosing a surjection

\mathcal{O}_{{\mathbb{D}}^{d}_{K}(0;\rho)\times_{K}\left]U\right[_{\mathfrak{P}}}^{\oplus m}\twoheadrightarrow\mathscr{F},

and once again appealing to left exactness, I can reduce to the case
\mathscr{F}=\mathcal{O}_{{\mathbb{D}}^{d}_{K}(0;\rho)\times_{K}\left]U\right[_{\mathfrak{P}}}
, in other words to showing that

\Gamma({\mathbb{D}}^{d}_{K}(0;\rho)\times_{K}\left]U\right[_{\mathfrak{P}},j^{\prime-1}i^{\prime}_{*}a_{*}\pi^{*}\mathscr{H})\rightarrow\underset{n}{\mathrm{lim}}\,\Gamma(\mathfrak{O}_{\mathfrak{P},K}\times_{\mathfrak{P}_{K}}\left]U\right[_{\mathfrak{P}},j^{-1}i_{*}a_{*}\pi_{n}^{*}\mathscr{H})

is injective. Writing things out explicitly, this is the map

\underset{\lambda}{\mathrm{colim}}\,\Gamma({\mathbb{D}}^{d}_{K}(0;\rho)\times_{K}(V_{\lambda}\cap\left]T\right[_{\mathfrak{P}}),\pi^{*}\mathscr{H})\rightarrow\underset{n}{\mathrm{lim}}\,\underset{\lambda}{\mathrm{colim}}\,\Gamma(\mathfrak{O}^{(n)}_{\mathfrak{P},K}\times_{\mathfrak{P}_{K}}(V_{\lambda}\cap\left]T\right[_{\mathfrak{P}}),\pi_{n}^{*}\mathscr{H}).

By an explicit calculation, I can identify

\Gamma(\mathfrak{O}^{(n)}_{\mathfrak{P},K}\times_{\mathfrak{P}_{K}}(V_{\lambda}\cap\left]T\right[_{\mathfrak{P}}),\pi_{n}^{*}\mathscr{H})=\Gamma(V_{\lambda}\cap\left]T\right[_{\mathfrak{P}},\mathscr{H})\otimes_{K}\frac{K[z_{1},\ldots,z_{d}]}{(z_{1},\ldots,z_{d})^{n+1}}.

Thus, by applying Lemma
1.7.1
, it suffices to show that:

(1)

for each sufficiently large
\lambda
, the map

\Gamma({\mathbb{D}}^{d}_{K}(0;\rho)\times_{K}(V_{\lambda}\cap\left]T\right[_{\mathfrak{P}}),\pi^{*}\mathscr{H})\rightarrow\underset{n}{\mathrm{lim}}\,\Gamma(\mathfrak{O}^{(n)}_{\mathfrak{P},K}\times_{\mathfrak{P}_{K}}(V_{\lambda}\cap\left]T\right[_{\mathfrak{P}}),\pi_{n}^{*}\mathscr{H})

is injective;

(2)

for each
\lambda
, the kernels of the transition maps

\Gamma(V_{\lambda}\cap\left]T\right[_{\mathfrak{P}},\mathscr{H})\rightarrow\Gamma(V_{\lambda^{\prime}}\cap\left]T\right[_{\mathfrak{P}},\mathscr{H})

eventually stabilise.

Since each
V_{\lambda}\cap\left]T\right[_{\mathfrak{P}}
has only finitely many connected components, the second of these follows from Lemma
1.4.5
.

For the first, note that the claim can be checked over an open cover of
\left]T\right[_{\mathfrak{P}}
. I am therefore again free to replace the (non-quasi-compact) open tube
\left]T\right[_{\mathfrak{P}}
by the (quasi-compact) closed tube
\left]T\right[_{\mathfrak{P}}\cap[\overline{T}]_{\mathfrak{P}\eta}
, where
\overline{T}
is the closure of
T
in
Y
.

Now choose a cofinal sequence
\{W_{\delta}\}_{\delta\geq\delta_{0}}
of open neighbourhoods of
\left]T\right[_{\mathfrak{P}}\cap[\overline{T}]_{\mathfrak{P}\eta}
in
[\overline{T}]_{\mathfrak{P}\eta}
. Then the spaces
{\mathbb{D}}^{d}_{K}(0;\rho)\times_{K}(V_{\lambda}\cap W_{\delta})
and
\mathfrak{O}^{(n)}_{\mathfrak{P},K}\times_{\mathfrak{P}_{K}}(V_{\lambda}\cap W_{\delta})
form cofinal sequences of neighbourhoods of

{\mathbb{D}}^{d}_{K}(0;\rho)\times_{K}(V_{\lambda}\cap\left]T\right[_{\mathfrak{P}}\cap[\overline{T}]_{\mathfrak{P}\eta})\text{ in }{\mathbb{D}}^{d}_{K}(0;\rho)\times_{K}(V_{\lambda}\cap[\overline{T}]_{\mathfrak{P}\eta})

and

\mathfrak{O}^{(n)}_{\mathfrak{P},K}\times_{\mathfrak{P}_{K}}(V_{\lambda}\cap\left]T\right[_{\mathfrak{P}}\cap[\overline{T}]_{\mathfrak{P}\eta})\text{ in }\mathfrak{O}^{(n)}_{\mathfrak{P},K}\times_{\mathfrak{P}_{K}}(V_{\lambda}\cap[\overline{T}]_{\mathfrak{P}\eta})

respectively. I may also assume, by increasing
\delta_{0}
if necessary, that
\mathscr{H}
extends to a locally free sheaf on
W_{\delta_{0}}
, and therefore on all
W_{\delta}
. I thus need to show that the map

\underset{\delta}{\mathrm{colim}}\,\Gamma({\mathbb{D}}^{d}_{K}(0;\rho)\times_{K}(V_{\lambda}\cap W_{\delta}),\pi^{*}\mathscr{H})\rightarrow\underset{n}{\mathrm{lim}}\,\underset{\delta}{\mathrm{colim}}\,\Gamma(\mathfrak{O}^{(n)}_{\mathfrak{P},K}\times_{\mathfrak{P}_{K}}(V_{\lambda}\cap W_{\delta}),\pi_{n}^{*}\mathscr{H})

is injective, at least for large enough
\lambda
. Again appealing to Lemma
1.7.1
, it is enough to prove that:

(A)

the map

(2.2.3)

\Gamma({\mathbb{D}}^{d}_{K}(0;\rho)\times_{K}(V_{\lambda}\cap W_{\delta}),\pi^{*}\mathscr{H})\rightarrow\underset{n}{\mathrm{lim}}\,\Gamma(\mathfrak{O}^{(n)}_{K}\times_{K}(V_{\lambda}\cap W_{\delta}),\pi_{n}^{*}\mathscr{H})

is injective;

(B)

for each
\eta
, the kernels of the transition maps

\Gamma(V_{\lambda}\cap W_{\delta},\mathscr{H})\rightarrow\Gamma(V_{\lambda}\cap W_{\delta^{\prime}},\mathscr{H})

eventually stabilise.

Again, the second of these follows from Lemma
1.4.5
, since each
V_{\lambda}\cap W_{\delta}
has only finitely many connected components. For the first, take an open affinoid
\mathrm{Spa}\left(R,R^{+}\right)\subset V_{\lambda}\cap W_{\delta}
, and set
M=\Gamma(\mathrm{Spa}\left(R,R^{+}\right),\mathscr{H})
. Thus
M
is a finite projective
R
-module. The given map can be identified with the map

R\langle\rho^{-1}\bm{z}\rangle\otimes_{R}M\rightarrow R\llbracket\bm{z}\rrbracket\otimes_{R}M

from the restricted power series ring to the full power series ring, tensored with
M
. Since this map is injective for each such
\mathrm{Spa}\left(R,R^{+}\right)
, the map (
2.2.3
) is injective as required.
∎

I can now show that constructible isocrystals can be detected over a stratification.

2.2.14 Proposition
.

Let
\{i_{\alpha}:X_{\alpha}\rightarrow X\}_{\alpha\in A}
be a stratification of
X
, and let
\mathscr{F}\in\mathrm{Mod}(\mathscr{D}_{\left]X\right[_{\mathfrak{P}}})
. Then
\mathscr{F}
is a constructible isocrystal if and only if, for each
\alpha\in A
,
i_{\alpha}^{-1}\mathscr{F}\in\mathrm{Mod}(\mathscr{D}_{\left]X_{\alpha}\right[_{\mathfrak{P}}})
is a constructible isocrystal.

Proof.

The ‘only if’ direction has already been proved. For the ‘if’ direction, by induction on
\left|A\right|
, I may assume that there are precisely two strata
\{U,Z\}
consisting of an open subscheme
j:U\rightarrow X
and a closed complement
i:Z\rightarrow X
. Thanks to Lemma
2.2.11
the property of being a constructible isocrystal is local on both
\mathfrak{P}
and
X
, so I may assume that both are affine. Moreover, I can appeal to Noetherian induction to shrink
U
if required. I can therefore assume that
U
is the complement of a hypersurface in
Y
, and that
j^{-1}\mathscr{F}
is a locally free isocrystal on
(U,Y,\mathfrak{P})
.

In this case, I will follow closely the proof of
[
LS14
, Proposition 5.11]
. If
T=X,U
or
Z
, I will write

\displaystyle p_{i}

\displaystyle\colon\left]T\right[_{\mathfrak{P}^{2}}\rightarrow\left]T\right[_{\mathfrak{P}}

\displaystyle p_{i}^{(n)}

\displaystyle\colon\left]T\right[_{\mathfrak{P}^{(n)}}\rightarrow\left]T\right[_{\mathfrak{P}}

for the projections, and if
\mathfrak{T}=\mathfrak{P},\mathfrak{P}^{2}
or
\mathfrak{P}^{(n)}
, I will write

i\colon\left]Z\right[_{\mathfrak{T}}\rightarrow\left]X\right[_{\mathfrak{T}}\leftarrow\left]U\right[_{\mathfrak{T}}\colon j

for the immersions. There are therefore isomorphisms

\epsilon_{U}:j^{-1}p_{1}^{*}\mathscr{F}\overset{\cong}{\longrightarrow}j^{-1}p_{0}^{*}\mathscr{F},\;\;\;\;\epsilon_{Z}:i^{-1}p_{1}^{*}\mathscr{F}\overset{\cong}{\longrightarrow}i^{-1}p_{0}^{*}\mathscr{F}

extending the Taylor isomorphisms on each
\left]X\right[_{\mathfrak{P}^{(n)}}
, and I need to show that these glue to an isomorphism
p_{1}^{*}\mathscr{F}\rightarrow p_{2}^{*}\mathscr{F}
on
\left]X\right[_{\mathfrak{P}^{2}}
.

To do this, I will use the fact that that sheaves on
\left]X\right[_{\mathfrak{P}^{2}}
are determined by their restriction to each of
\left]U\right[_{\mathfrak{P}^{2}}
and
\left]Z\right[_{\mathfrak{P}^{2}}
, together with the natural adjunction morphism between then. Moreover, this construction gives rise to an equivalence of categories. Thus
\epsilon_{U}
and
\epsilon_{Z}
glue to give the required stratification if and only if the diagram

\textstyle{j^{-1}p_{1}^{*}\mathscr{F}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\epsilon_{U}}
\textstyle{j^{-1}i_{*}i^{-1}p_{1}^{*}\mathscr{F}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\epsilon_{Z}}
\textstyle{j^{-1}p_{0}^{*}\mathscr{F}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{j^{-1}i_{*}i^{-1}p_{0}^{*}\mathscr{F}}

commutes. Equivalently, this happens if and only if the difference between the two maps

j^{-1}p_{1}^{*}\mathscr{F}\rightarrow j^{-1}i_{*}i^{-1}p_{0}^{*}\mathscr{F}

is zero. Note that the analogous diagram

\textstyle{j^{-1}p_{1}^{(n)*}\mathscr{F}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\epsilon_{U}}
\textstyle{j^{-1}i_{*}i^{-1}p_{1}^{(n)*}\mathscr{F}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\epsilon_{Z}}
\textstyle{j^{-1}p_{0}^{(n)*}\mathscr{F}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{j^{-1}i_{*}i^{-1}p_{0}^{(n)*}\mathscr{F}}

commutes, for all
n
, so it suffices to show that the natural map

(2.2.4)

\mathrm{Hom}_{\mathcal{O}_{\left]U\right[_{\mathfrak{P}^{2}}}}(j^{-1}p_{1}^{*}\mathscr{F},j^{-1}i_{*}i^{-1}p_{0}^{*}\mathscr{F})\rightarrow\underset{n}{\mathrm{lim}}\,\mathrm{Hom}_{\mathcal{O}_{\left]U\right[_{\mathfrak{P}^{(n)}}}}(j^{-1}p_{1}^{(n)*}\mathscr{F},j^{-1}i_{*}i^{-1}p_{0}^{(n)*}\mathscr{F})

is injective. Since
\mathfrak{P}
is smooth over
\mathcal{V}
in a neighbourhood of
X
, I can now appeal to Berthelot’s strong fibration theorem (see in particular the form stated in Theorem
1.4.2
above). This says that, after localising on
X
and
\mathfrak{P}
, I can replace the morphism of frames

p_{0}:(X,Y,\mathfrak{P}^{2})\rightarrow(X,Y,\mathfrak{P})

by the natural projection

\pi:(X,Y,\widehat{{\mathbb{A}}}^{d}_{\mathfrak{P}})\rightarrow(X,Y,\mathfrak{P}),

where
d
is the relative dimension of
\mathfrak{P}
over
\mathcal{V}
around
X
, and
Y
is embedded into
\widehat{{\mathbb{A}}}^{d}_{\mathfrak{P}}
via the zero section. Since
\mathfrak{P}^{(n)}
now gets identified with the
n
th infinitesimal neighbourhood of the zero section
\mathfrak{P}\rightarrow\widehat{{\mathbb{A}}}^{d}_{\mathfrak{P}}
, the claim is now precisely the one proved in Lemma
2.2.13
above.
∎

2.2.15 Corollary
.

The subcategory
\mathbf{Isoc}_{\mathrm{cons}}(X,Y,\mathfrak{P})\subset\mathbf{Mod}(\mathscr{D}_{\left]X\right[_{\mathfrak{P}}})
is closed under extensions.

Proof.

Let

0\rightarrow\mathscr{F}\rightarrow\mathscr{G}\rightarrow\mathscr{H}\rightarrow 0

be a short exact sequence of
\mathscr{D}_{\left]X\right[_{\mathfrak{P}}}
-modules, with both
\mathscr{F},\mathscr{H}\in\mathbf{Isoc}_{\mathrm{cons}}(X,Y,\mathfrak{P})
. Thanks to Proposition
2.2.14
, it suffices to find a stratification of
X
over which
\mathscr{G}
becomes a constructible isocrystal. To do so, choose stratifications of
X
over which
\mathscr{F}
and
\mathscr{H}
become locally free isocrystals, and take a common refinement. This gives a stratification over which both
\mathscr{F}
and
\mathscr{H}
become locally free isocrystals, in which case it is proved in
[
CLS99
, Proposition 1.2.2]
that
\mathscr{G}
is a locally free isocrystal.
∎

I leave for now the question of to what extent the category
\mathbf{Isoc}_{\mathrm{cons}}(X,Y,\mathfrak{P})
is independent of
Y
and
\mathfrak{P}
, since I shall shortly deduce it from a more general invariance result for the derived analogue of these categories.

3.
The derived category of constructible isocrystals

In §
2
above I introduced abelian categories of constructible isocrystals. The next task is to establish the basic properties of the analogous triangulated categories.

3.1.
Triangulated categories of constructible isocrystals

Let
(X,Y,\mathfrak{P})
be a frame, with
\mathfrak{P}
smooth around
X
. The following is the derived analogue of Definition
2.2.3
.

3.1.1 Definition
.

A constructible complex on
(X,Y,\mathfrak{P})
is a complex
\mathscr{K}\in{\bf D}^{b}(\mathscr{D}_{\left]X\right[_{\mathfrak{P}}})
whose cohomology sheaves are constructible isocrystals. We define
{\bf D}^{b}_{\mathrm{cons}}(X,Y,\mathfrak{P})
to be the corresponding full subcategory of
{\bf D}^{b}(\mathscr{D}_{\left]X\right[_{\mathfrak{P}}})
.

3.1.2 Remark
.

(1)

Note that if
\mathfrak{P}
is everywhere smooth, and
i:X\rightarrow P
is the given immersion, then the extension by zero functor

i_{!}:{\bf D}^{b}(\mathscr{D}_{\left]X\right[_{\mathfrak{P}}})\rightarrow{\bf D}^{b}(\mathscr{D}_{\mathfrak{P}_{K}})

is fully faithful, with essential image those constructible complexes supported on
X
(see Remark
2.2.7
).

(2)

Again, if
X=Y=P
, I will generally write
{\bf D}^{b}_{\mathrm{cons}}(\mathfrak{P})
instead of
{\bf D}^{b}_{\mathrm{cons}}(P,P,\mathfrak{P})
.

3.1.3 Lemma
.

The subcategory
{\bf D}^{b}_{\mathrm{cons}}(X,Y,\mathfrak{P})\subset{\bf D}^{b}(\mathscr{D}_{\left]X\right[_{\mathfrak{P}}})
is triangulated. Moreover, it is the smallest triangulated subcategory containing all locally free isocrystals supported on locally closed subschemes of
X
.

Proof.

This follows from Corollaries
2.2.9
and
2.2.15
.
∎

Since constructible isocrystals are
\mathcal{O}_{\left]X\right[_{\mathfrak{P}}}
-flat by Lemma
2.2.4
, and stable under tensor product over
\mathcal{O}_{\left]X\right[_{\mathfrak{P}}}
, this tensor product descends to a t-exact functor

-\otimes_{\mathcal{O}_{\left]X\right[_{\mathfrak{P}}}}-:{\bf D}^{b}_{\mathrm{cons}}(X,Y,\mathfrak{P})\times{\bf D}^{b}_{\mathrm{cons}}(X,Y,\mathfrak{P})\rightarrow{\bf D}^{b}_{\mathrm{cons}}(X,Y,\mathfrak{P}).

Similarly, if
(f,g,u):(X^{\prime},Y^{\prime},\mathfrak{P}^{\prime})\rightarrow(X,Y,\mathfrak{P})
is a morphism of frames, the pullback functor
\left]f\right[_{u}^{*}
from
\mathscr{D}_{\left]X\right[_{\mathfrak{P}}}
-modules to
\mathscr{D}_{\left]X^{\prime}\right[_{\mathfrak{P}^{\prime}}}
-modules preserves constructible isocrystals and derives trivially to give a t-exact functor

\left]f\right[_{u}^{*}:{\bf D}^{b}_{\mathrm{cons}}(X,Y,\mathfrak{P})\rightarrow{\bf D}^{b}_{\mathrm{cons}}(X^{\prime},Y^{\prime},\mathfrak{P}^{\prime}).

Pullback and tensor product commute in the obvious sense.

3.2.
Independence of the frame

The key point will be to show that
{\bf D}^{b}_{\mathrm{cons}}(X,Y,\mathfrak{P})
enjoys the same functorial properties as classical categories of (locally free) isocrystals
\mathbf{Isoc}(X,Y,\mathfrak{P})
. As in that case, the crucial invariance result is the following.

3.2.1 Theorem
.

Let

\textstyle{Y^{\prime}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{g}
\textstyle{\mathfrak{P}^{\prime}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{u}
\textstyle{X\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces Y}
\textstyle{\mathfrak{P}}

be a morphism of frames, such that
g
is proper, and both
\mathfrak{P}
and
u
are smooth around
X
. Let
d
be the relative dimension of
u
, and write
u=\left]\mathrm{id}\right[_{u}
for the induced morphism
\left]X\right[_{\mathfrak{P}^{\prime}}\rightarrow\left]X\right[_{\mathfrak{P}}
. Then the functor

u^{*}\colon{\bf D}(\mathscr{D}_{\left]X\right[_{\mathfrak{P}}})\rightarrow{\bf D}(\mathscr{D}_{\left]X\right[_{\mathfrak{P}^{\prime}}})

induces an t-exact equivalence of categories

{\bf D}^{b}_{\mathrm{cons}}(X,Y,\mathfrak{P})\rightarrow{\bf D}^{b}_{\mathrm{cons}}(X,Y,\mathfrak{P}^{\prime}).

The functors

\displaystyle\mathbf{R}u_{\mathrm{dR}!}[2d]

\displaystyle\colon{\bf D}(\mathscr{D}_{\left]X\right[_{\mathfrak{P}^{\prime}}})\rightarrow{\bf D}(\mathscr{D}_{\left]X\right[_{\mathfrak{P}}})

\displaystyle\mathbf{R}u_{\mathrm{dR}*}

\displaystyle\colon{\bf D}(\mathscr{D}_{\left]X\right[_{\mathfrak{P}^{\prime}}})\rightarrow{\bf D}(\mathscr{D}_{\left]X\right[_{\mathfrak{P}}})

both induce quasi-inverses to
u^{*}
.

To begin the proof of Theorem
3.2.1
, I make the following simple observation.

3.2.2 Lemma
.

The functor
u^{*}
sends
{\bf D}^{b}_{\mathrm{cons}}(X,Y,\mathfrak{P})
into
{\bf D}^{b}_{\mathrm{cons}}(X,Y^{\prime},\mathfrak{P}^{\prime})
, and the essential image contains all locally free isocrystals supported on locally closed subschemes of
X
.

Proof.

The first claim has already been observed in §
3.1
above. The second claim is then a consequence of the following classical result, proved for example in
[
LS07
, Theorem 7.1.8]
: for any locally closed subscheme
i:Z\hookrightarrow X
, with closures
\overline{Z}
in
Y
and
\overline{Z}^{\prime}
in
Y^{\prime}
, the pullback functor

u^{*}:\mathbf{Isoc}(Z,\overline{Z},\mathfrak{P})\rightarrow\mathbf{Isoc}(Z,\overline{Z}^{\prime},\mathfrak{P}^{\prime})

induced by the (abusively denoted) morphism

u\colon\left]Z\right[_{\mathfrak{P}^{\prime}}\rightarrow\left]Z\right[_{\mathfrak{P}}

on locally free isocrystals is an equivalence of categories. Indeed, if
\mathscr{F}
is a locally free isocrystal on
(Z,\overline{Z}^{\prime},\mathfrak{P}^{\prime})
, then
\mathscr{F}=u^{*}\mathscr{G}
for some locally free isocrystal on
(Z,\overline{Z},\mathfrak{P})
. It then follows from Lemma
1.2.6
that
i_{!}\mathscr{F}=i_{!}u^{*}\mathscr{G}=u^{*}i_{!}\mathscr{G}
is in the essential image of
u^{*}
.
∎

Proving Theorem
3.2.1
eventually boils down to a local calculation in the same situation as that considered in Lemma
2.2.13
.

3.2.3 Proposition
.

Consider Setup
2.2.12
, and suppose that
\mathscr{F},\mathscr{G}\in\mathbf{Mod}(\mathscr{D}_{\left]X\right[_{\mathfrak{P}}})
are constructible as
\mathcal{O}_{\left]X\right[_{\mathfrak{P}}}
-modules.

(1)

The natural map
\mathscr{F}\rightarrow\mathbf{R}\pi_{\mathrm{dR}*}\pi^{*}\mathscr{F}
is an isomorphism in
{\bf D}(\mathscr{D}_{\left]X\right[_{\mathfrak{P}}})
.

(2)

The natural map

\mathbf{R}\mathrm{Hom}_{\mathscr{D}_{\left]X\right[_{\mathfrak{P}}}}(\mathscr{F},\mathscr{G})\rightarrow\mathbf{R}\mathrm{Hom}_{\mathscr{D}_{\left]X\right[_{\widehat{{\mathbb{A}}}^{d}_{\mathfrak{P}}}}}(\pi^{*}\mathscr{F},\pi^{*}\mathscr{G})

is an isomorphism in
{\bf D}(K)
.

3.2.4 Remark
.

Note that the hypotheses of Theorem
3.2.1
have been weakened slightly here. First of all, I only require that
\mathfrak{P}
is rig-smooth, rather than smooth, in a neighbourhood of
X
. Secondly, I only require that
\mathscr{F}
and
\mathscr{G}
, are constructible, and not necessarily convergent. This implies that the full faithfulness part of Theorem
3.2.1
holds under similarly weaker hypotheses. The essential surjectivity, of course, does not.

Proof.

(1)

First of all, the claim is local on
\left]Y\right[_{\mathfrak{P}}
, and so, replacing
\mathfrak{P}
by a formal model for the closed tube
[Y]_{\mathfrak{P}\eta}
, I can assume that
Y=P
. Letting
j\colon X\rightarrow P
denote the given open immersion, the claim for
\mathscr{F}
on
\left]X\right[_{\mathfrak{P}}
is equivalent to the claim for
j_{*}\mathscr{F}
on
\mathfrak{P}_{K}
by Lemma
1.2.6
, so I can also assume that
X=P
.

Now, for any
\rho<1
, let
\pi_{\rho}:{\mathbb{D}}^{d}_{\mathfrak{P}_{K}}(0;\rho)\rightarrow\mathfrak{P}_{K}
denote the projection, thus

\mathbf{R}\pi_{\mathrm{dR}*}\pi^{*}\mathscr{F}\overset{\cong}{\longrightarrow}\mathbf{R}\underset{\rho}{\mathrm{lim}}\,{\bf R}\pi_{\rho\mathrm{dR}*}\pi_{\rho}^{*}\mathscr{F}.

Since
\pi_{\rho}
is quasi-compact, it follows from Proposition
2.1.6
that

{\bf R}\pi_{\rho\mathrm{dR}*}\mathcal{O}_{{\mathbb{D}}^{d}_{\mathfrak{P}_{K}}(0;\rho)}\otimes_{\mathcal{O}_{\mathfrak{P}_{K}}}\mathscr{F}\overset{\cong}{\longrightarrow}{\bf R}\pi_{\rho\mathrm{dR}*}\pi_{\rho}^{*}\mathscr{F},

thus

\mathbf{R}\pi_{\mathrm{dR}*}\pi^{*}\mathscr{F}\overset{\cong}{\longrightarrow}\mathbf{R}\underset{\rho}{\mathrm{lim}}\,\left({\bf R}\pi_{\rho\mathrm{dR}*}\mathcal{O}_{{\mathbb{D}}^{d}_{\mathfrak{P}_{K}}(0;\rho)}\otimes_{\mathcal{O}_{\mathfrak{P}_{K}}}\mathscr{F}\right).

Next, let
\pi_{\rho^{-}}:{\mathbb{D}}^{d}_{\mathfrak{P}_{K}}(0;\rho^{-})\rightarrow\mathfrak{P}_{K}
denote the projection from the
open
disc, thus

\mathbf{R}\underset{\rho}{\mathrm{lim}}\,\left({\bf R}\pi_{\rho\mathrm{dR}*}\mathcal{O}_{{\mathbb{D}}^{d}_{\mathfrak{P}_{K}}(0;\rho)}\otimes_{\mathcal{O}_{\mathfrak{P}_{K}}}\mathscr{F}\right)\overset{\cong}{\longrightarrow}\mathbf{R}\underset{\rho}{\mathrm{lim}}\,\left({\bf R}\pi_{\rho^{-}\mathrm{dR}*}\mathcal{O}_{{\mathbb{D}}^{d}_{\mathfrak{P}_{K}}(0;\rho^{-})}\otimes_{\mathcal{O}_{\mathfrak{P}_{K}}}\mathscr{F}\right).

It therefore suffices to show that

\mathcal{O}_{\mathfrak{P}_{K}}\overset{\cong}{\longrightarrow}{\bf R}\pi_{\rho^{-}\mathrm{dR}*}\mathcal{O}_{{\mathbb{D}}^{d}_{\mathfrak{P}_{K}}(0;\rho^{-})},

which is a well-known calculation in rigid analytic geometry.

(2)

This is a simple consequence of adjunction and part (
1
):

\mathbf{R}\mathrm{Hom}_{\mathscr{D}_{\left]X\right[_{\widehat{{\mathbb{A}}}^{d}_{\mathfrak{P}}}}}(\pi^{*}\mathscr{F},\pi^{*}\mathscr{G})=\mathbf{R}\mathrm{Hom}_{\mathscr{D}_{\left]X\right[_{\mathfrak{P}}}}(\mathscr{F},\mathbf{R}\pi_{\mathrm{dR}*}\pi^{*}\mathscr{G})=\mathbf{R}\mathrm{Hom}_{\mathscr{D}_{\left]X\right[_{\mathfrak{P}}}}(\mathscr{F},\mathscr{G}).\qed

Proof of Theorem
3.2.1
.

First consider the claim that
u^{*}
induces an equivalence of categories

{\bf D}^{b}_{\mathrm{cons}}(X,Y,\mathfrak{P})\rightarrow{\bf D}^{b}_{\mathrm{cons}}(X,Y^{\prime},\mathfrak{P}^{\prime}).

To prove this, it suffices to show that the map

(3.2.1)

\mathbf{R}\mathrm{Hom}_{\mathscr{D}_{\left]X\right[_{\mathfrak{P}}}}(\mathscr{F},\mathscr{G})\rightarrow\mathbf{R}\mathrm{Hom}_{\mathscr{D}_{\left]X\right[_{\mathfrak{P}^{\prime}}}}(u^{*}\mathscr{F},u^{*}\mathscr{G})

is an isomorphism. Indeed, this immediately implies that
u^{*}
is fully faithful, but in conjunction with Lemmas
3.1.3
and
3.2.2
, it also implies that
u^{*}
is essentially surjective, since the essential image is triangulated and contains all locally free isocrystals supported on locally closed subschemes of
X
.

To prove this ‘derived full faithfulness’, I can clearly localise on
\mathfrak{P}
, and I claim that I can also localise on
X
. To see this, cover
X
by open subspaces
\{X_{i}\}_{1\leq i\leq n}
, and for any
I\subset\{1,\ldots,n\}
set
X_{I}=\cap_{i\in I}X_{i}
. Then, as in
[
Ber96a
, Proposition 2.1.8]
, any sheaf
\mathscr{E}
on
\left]X\right[_{\mathfrak{P}}
has a canonical resolution

0\rightarrow\mathscr{E}\rightarrow\bigoplus_{i=1}^{n}j_{X_{i}}^{\dagger}\mathscr{E}\rightarrow\ldots\rightarrow j^{\dagger}_{X_{\{1,\ldots,n\}}}\mathscr{E}\rightarrow 0.

Applying this to the complexes
\mathscr{F}
and
\mathscr{G}
, it follows that we can deduce the isomorphy of (
3.2.1
) from the isomorphy of

(3.2.2)

\mathbf{R}\mathrm{Hom}_{\mathscr{D}_{\left]X\right[_{\mathfrak{P}}}}(j_{X_{I}}^{\dagger}\mathscr{F},j_{X_{J}}^{\dagger}\mathscr{G})\rightarrow\mathbf{R}\mathrm{Hom}_{\mathscr{D}_{\left]X\right[_{\mathfrak{P}^{\prime}}}}(u^{*}j_{X_{I}}^{\dagger}\mathscr{F},u^{*}j_{X_{J}}^{\dagger}\mathscr{G})

for any pair of non-empty open subset
I,J\subset\{1,\ldots,n\}
. Set
\mathscr{F}^{\prime}=(j_{X_{I}}^{\dagger}\mathscr{F})|_{\left]X_{J}\right[_{\mathfrak{P}}}
, and
\mathscr{G}^{\prime}=\mathscr{G}|_{\left]X_{J}\right[_{\mathfrak{P}}}
, these are constructible complexes on
(X_{J},Y,\mathfrak{P})
. Applying Lemma
1.2.6
identifies (
3.2.2
) with the map

\mathbf{R}\mathrm{Hom}_{\mathscr{D}_{\left]X_{J}\right[_{\mathfrak{P}}}}(\mathscr{F}^{\prime},\mathscr{G}^{\prime})\rightarrow\mathbf{R}\mathrm{Hom}_{\mathscr{D}_{\left]X_{J}\right[_{\mathfrak{P}^{\prime}}}}(u^{*}\mathscr{F}^{\prime},u^{*}\mathscr{G}^{\prime}).

Hence the claim for each
X_{J}
implies the claim for
X
.

If
g
is an isomorphism, then by Theorem
1.4.2
, we can see that, after localising on
X
and
\mathfrak{P}
, there exists an isomorphism

\left]X\right[_{\mathfrak{P}^{\prime}}\overset{\cong}{\longrightarrow}{\mathbb{D}}^{d}_{K}(0;1^{-})\times_{K}\left]X\right[_{\mathfrak{P}}

of germs identifying
u
with the second projection. Hence applying Proposition
3.2.3
shows that (
3.2.1
) is an isomorphism.

In general I now argue as in
[
LS07
, Theorem 7.1.8]
. Indeed, it follows from what I have already proved that if
(X,Y)
is a weakly realisable pair (in the sense of Definition
1.2.3
), and
(X,Y,\mathfrak{P})
is a frame with
\mathfrak{P}
smooth around
X
, then
{\bf D}^{b}_{\mathrm{cons}}(X,Y,\mathfrak{P})
only depends on
(X,Y)
and not on
\mathfrak{P}
, and is moreover functorial in the pair
(X,Y)
. I can therefore denote
{\bf D}^{b}_{\mathrm{cons}}(X,Y,\mathfrak{P})
by
{\bf D}^{b}_{\mathrm{cons}}(X,Y)
, and the claim amounts to showing that if
({\rm id},g)\colon(X,Y^{\prime})\rightarrow(X,Y)
is a morphism of weakly realisable pairs, with
g
proper, then
g^{*}\colon{\bf D}^{b}_{\mathrm{cons}}(X,Y)\rightarrow{\bf D}^{b}_{\mathrm{cons}}(X,Y^{\prime})
is an equivalence, or indeed ‘derived fully faithful’ in the sense that the analogue of (
3.2.1
) is an isomorphism. The question is local on
Y
, which I may therefore assume to be quasi-projective, and thus by Chow’s lemma there exists a projective morphism
g^{\prime}\colon Y^{\prime\prime}\rightarrow Y^{\prime}
such that
g\circ g^{\prime}
is also projective. Thus I can reduce to the case that
g
is projective. Hence by
[
LS07
, Lemma 6.5.1]

({\rm id},g)
extends to a morphism of frames

\textstyle{Y^{\prime}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{g}
\textstyle{\mathfrak{P}^{\prime}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{u}
\textstyle{X\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{Y\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{\mathfrak{P}}

such that
u
is étale around
X
. Hence
u\colon\left]X\right[_{\mathfrak{P}^{\prime}}\rightarrow\left]X\right[_{\mathfrak{P}}
is an isomorphism by Theorem
1.4.2
, and so
u^{*}
is trivially ‘derived fully faithful’.

Next, consider the claims that both
{\bf R}u_{\mathrm{dR}*}
and
{\bf R}u_{\mathrm{dR}!}[2d]
are quasi-inverses to
u^{*}
. To begin with,

{\bf R}u_{\mathrm{dR}*}\colon{\bf D}^{b}(\mathscr{D}_{\left]X\right[_{\mathfrak{P}^{\prime}}})\rightarrow{\bf D}^{b}(\mathscr{D}_{\left]X\right[_{\mathfrak{P}}})

is right adjoint to
u^{*}
. Thus I can argue exactly as above (that is, following the proof of
[
LS07
, Theorem 7.1.8]
) to show that the map

\mathrm{id}\overset{\cong}{\longrightarrow}{\bf R}u_{\mathrm{dR}*}u^{*}

is an isomorphism on objects of
{\bf D}^{b}_{\mathrm{cons}}(X,Y,\mathfrak{P})
, by reducing to Proposition
3.2.3
(
1
). It thus follows that
{\bf R}u_{\mathrm{dR}*}
does indeed descend to a quasi-inverse

{\bf D}^{b}_{\mathrm{cons}}(X,Y^{\prime},\mathfrak{P}^{\prime})\rightarrow{\bf D}^{b}_{\mathrm{cons}}(X,Y,\mathfrak{P})

to
u^{*}
.

To prove the same is true for
\mathbf{R}u_{\mathrm{dR}!}[2d]
, Proposition
2.1.6
shows that there is, for any
\mathscr{F}\in{\bf D}^{b}_{\mathrm{cons}}(X,Y,\mathfrak{P})
, an isomorphism

(3.2.3)

\mathscr{F}\otimes_{\mathcal{O}_{\left]X\right[_{\mathfrak{P}}}}\mathbf{R}u_{\mathrm{dR}!}\mathcal{O}_{\left]X\right[_{\mathfrak{P}^{\prime}}}\overset{\cong}{\longrightarrow}\mathbf{R}u_{\mathrm{dR}!}u^{*}\mathscr{F}

in
{\bf D}^{b}(\mathscr{D}_{\left]X\right[_{\mathfrak{P}}})
. It follows from Corollary
1.5.1
that the trace map gives an isomorphism

\mathrm{Tr}\colon\mathbf{R}u_{\mathrm{dR}!}\mathcal{O}_{\left]X\right[_{\mathfrak{P}^{\prime}}}[2d]\rightarrow\mathcal{O}_{\left]X\right[_{\mathfrak{P}}},

and therefore an isomorphism

\mathbf{R}u_{\mathrm{dR}!}u^{*}\mathscr{F}[2d]\overset{\cong}{\longrightarrow}\mathscr{F}

for any
\mathscr{F}\in{\bf D}^{b}_{\mathrm{cons}}(X,Y,\mathfrak{P})
. This completes the proof.
∎

3.2.5 Remark
.

In the situation of Theorem
3.2.1
, suppose in addition that the morphism of frames
u\colon(X,Y^{\prime},\mathfrak{P}^{\prime})\rightarrow(X,Y,\mathfrak{P})
admits a section
v\colon(X,Y,\mathfrak{P})\rightarrow(X,Y^{\prime},\mathfrak{P}^{\prime})
. Then
v^{*}
is a left inverse to
u^{*}
, and hence an inverse to
u^{*}
. In particular, it follows that

v^{*}\cong\mathbf{R}u_{\mathrm{dR}*}\cong{\bf R}u_{\mathrm{dR}!}[2d]

as functors
{\bf D}^{b}_{\mathrm{cons}}(X,Y^{\prime},\mathfrak{P}^{\prime})\rightarrow{\bf D}^{b}_{\mathrm{cons}}(X,Y,\mathfrak{P})
.

3.2.6 Corollary
.

In the situation of Theorem
3.2.1
, the functor

u^{*}\colon\mathbf{Isoc}_{\mathrm{cons}}(X,Y,\mathfrak{P})\rightarrow\mathbf{Isoc}_{\mathrm{cons}}(X,Y^{\prime},\mathfrak{P}^{\prime})

is an equivalence of categories, with quasi-inverse given by either
{\bf R}u_{\mathrm{dR}*}
or
{\bf R}u_{\mathrm{dR}!}[2d]
.

3.2.7 Corollary
.

The categories
\mathbf{Isoc}_{\mathrm{cons}}(X,Y,\mathfrak{P})
and
{\bf D}^{b}_{\mathrm{cons}}(X,Y,\mathfrak{P})
are independent of
\mathfrak{P}
up to canonical equivalence, and functorial in
(X,Y)
.

I may therefore denote then by
\mathbf{Isoc}_{\mathrm{cons}}(X,Y)
and
{\bf D}^{b}_{\mathrm{cons}}(X,Y)
respectively. Similarly there is the full subcategory
\mathbf{Isoc}(X,Y)\subset\mathbf{Isoc}_{\mathrm{cons}}(X,Y)
of locally free isocrystals.

3.2.8 Corollary
.

If
(\mathrm{id},g)\colon(X,Y^{\prime})\rightarrow(X,Y)
is a morphism of pairs, with
g
proper, then

g^{*}\colon{\bf D}^{b}_{\mathrm{cons}}(X,Y)\rightarrow{\bf D}^{b}_{\mathrm{cons}}(X,Y^{\prime})

is a t-exact equivalence of categories. It follows that if
Y
is proper, then
\mathbf{Isoc}_{\mathrm{cons}}(X,Y)
and
{\bf D}^{b}_{\mathrm{cons}}(X,Y)
are independent of
Y
up to canonical equivalence, and functorial in
X
.

I will therefore denote these categories by
\mathbf{Isoc}_{\mathrm{cons}}(X)
and
{\bf D}^{b}_{\mathrm{cons}}(X)
respectively, again there is the full subcategory
\mathbf{Isoc}(X)\subset\mathbf{Isoc}_{\mathrm{cons}}(X)
of locally free isocrystals. Note that these are all categories of
overconvergent
objects on
X
. In particular, what I write as
\mathbf{Isoc}(X)
is the category of
overconvergent
isocrystals on
X
, and is more commonly written
\mathbf{Isoc}^{\dagger}(X)
. In my notation, the category of
convergent
isocrystals on
X
is written
\mathbf{Isoc}(X,X)
.
15
15

15

Another natural choice for the category of convergent isocrystals on
X
might be
\mathbf{Isoc}^{\circ}(X)
, using the fact that passing from
\mathbf{Isoc}(X)
to
\mathbf{Isoc}^{\circ}(X)
involves restricting to the
interior
of
\left]X\right[_{\mathfrak{P}}
(as a subset of
\left]Y\right[_{\mathfrak{P}}
). I will use this notation later on for log convergent isocrystals.
Unfortunately, this does slightly clash with the notation
\mathbf{Isoc}_{\mathrm{cons}}(\mathfrak{P})
used above for smooth formal schemes
\mathfrak{P}
, thus

\mathbf{Isoc}_{\mathrm{cons}}(\mathfrak{P})=\mathbf{Isoc}_{\mathrm{cons}}(P,P)\neq\mathbf{Isoc}_{\mathrm{cons}}(P).

The reader should therefore bear in mind that when
\mathfrak{P}
is a smooth formal scheme,
\mathbf{Isoc}_{(\mathrm{cons})}(\mathfrak{P})
means the category of
convergent
(constructible) isocrystals on
\mathfrak{P}
(or equivalently
P
), whereas when
X
is a variety,
\mathbf{Isoc}_{(\mathrm{cons})}(X)
will mean the category of
overconvergent
(constructible) isocrystals on
X
.

Anyway, if
(f,g)\colon(X^{\prime},Y^{\prime})\rightarrow(X,Y)
is a morphism of pairs, I will usually write

\displaystyle f^{*}\colon

\displaystyle\mathbf{Isoc}_{\mathrm{cons}}(X,Y)\rightarrow\mathbf{Isoc}_{\mathrm{cons}}(X^{\prime},Y^{\prime})

\displaystyle f^{*}\colon

\displaystyle{\bf D}^{b}_{\mathrm{cons}}(X,Y)\rightarrow{\bf D}^{b}_{\mathrm{cons}}(X^{\prime},Y^{\prime})

for the pullback functors, and similarly for morphisms of varieties over
k
. If
g
is a closed immersion, I will also write

\displaystyle f_{!}\colon

\displaystyle\mathbf{Isoc}_{\mathrm{cons}}(X^{\prime},Y^{\prime})\rightarrow\mathbf{Isoc}_{\mathrm{cons}}(X,Y)

\displaystyle f_{!}\colon

\displaystyle{\bf D}^{b}_{\mathrm{cons}}(X^{\prime},Y^{\prime})\rightarrow{\bf D}^{b}_{\mathrm{cons}}(X,Y)

for the (t-)exact functors which, on realisations, is the extension by zero along the inclusion
\left]X^{\prime}\right[_{\mathfrak{P}}\rightarrow\left]X\right[_{\mathfrak{P}}
of the locally closed subspace
\left]X^{\prime}\right[_{\mathfrak{P}}
of
\left]X\right[_{\mathfrak{P}}
. Note that when
f
is an open immersion, these are right adjoint to
f^{*}
, and when
f
is a closed immersion, they are left adjoint to
f^{*}
.

It is a straightforward check that the tensor product
\otimes_{\mathcal{O}_{\left]X\right[_{\mathfrak{P}}}}
descends to a well-defined functor on either of
{\bf D}^{b}_{\mathrm{cons}}(X,Y)
or
{\bf D}^{b}_{\mathrm{cons}}(X)
, which I will denote by
\otimes_{\mathcal{O}_{X}}
.

3.3.
Frobenius structures

Functoriality in
(X,Y)
now means that it makes sense to talk about Frobenius structures on constructible isocrystals and complexes. Indeed, the absolute Frobenius morphism
F\colon(X,Y)\rightarrow(X,Y)
induces a
\sigma
-linear pullback functor

F^{*}\colon\mathbf{Isoc}_{\mathrm{cons}}(X,Y,\mathfrak{P})\rightarrow\mathbf{Isoc}_{\mathrm{cons}}(X,Y,\mathfrak{P}).

3.3.1 Definition
.

A Frobenius structure on an object
\mathscr{F}\in\mathbf{Isoc}_{\mathrm{cons}}(X,Y,\mathfrak{P})
is an isomorphism

\varphi\colon F^{n*}\mathscr{F}\overset{\cong}{\longrightarrow}\mathscr{F}

for some
n
. An object
\mathscr{F}\in\mathbf{Isoc}_{\mathrm{cons}}(X,Y,\mathfrak{P})
is said to be of Frobenius type if it is an iterated extension of objects admitting a Frobenius structure.

The reader should be warned that again, this terminology is not completely standard, and what I have termed ‘of Frobenius type’ has been previously referred to as ‘
F
-able’ in the literature. The terminology here matches that used in
[
AL22
]
, however, is different from that used in
[
LS14
]
. Anyway, I will write
\mathbf{Isoc}_{\mathrm{cons},F}(X,Y,\mathfrak{P})\subset\mathbf{Isoc}_{\mathrm{cons}}(X,Y,\mathfrak{P})
for the full subcategory consisting of objects which are of Frobenius type. There are similarly defined categories
\mathbf{Isoc}_{\mathrm{cons},F}(X,Y)
,
\mathbf{Isoc}_{\mathrm{cons},F}(X)
and
\mathbf{Isoc}_{\mathrm{cons},F}(\mathfrak{P})
, as well as the obvious analogues for categories of locally free isocrystals.

3.3.2 Definition
.

A complex
\mathscr{K}\in{\bf D}^{b}_{\mathrm{cons}}(X,Y,\mathfrak{P})
is said to be of Frobenius type if all of its cohomology sheaves are. I will denote by

{\bf D}^{b}_{\mathrm{cons},F}(X,Y,\mathfrak{P})\subset{\bf D}^{b}_{\mathrm{cons}}(X,Y,\mathfrak{P})

the full subcategory consisting of objects of Frobenius type.

I will similarly write
{\bf D}^{b}_{\mathrm{cons},F}(X,Y)
,
{\bf D}^{b}_{\mathrm{cons},F}(X)
and
{\bf D}^{b}_{\mathrm{cons},F}(\mathfrak{P})
for the analogous categories associated to pairs, varieties, and formal schemes.

3.4.
Finite étale pushfowards and pullbacks

There is one last general result I will need on constructible isocrystals, and that is a version of Theorem
3.2.1
in a slightly more general setting. Instead of having two different frames enclosing a single variety
X
, I will need to consider a morphism of frames inducing a finite étale cover of
X
. That is, a morphism

\textstyle{X^{\prime}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{f}
\textstyle{Y^{\prime}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{g}
\textstyle{\mathfrak{P}^{\prime}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{u}
\textstyle{X\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{Y\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{\mathfrak{P}}

such that
\mathfrak{P}
is smooth around
X
,
u
is smooth around
X^{\prime}
of relative dimension
d
,
g
is proper, and
f
is finite étale. For locally free isocrystals, the result I require can be proved rather abstractly.

3.4.1 Proposition
.

The functor

f^{*}\colon\mathbf{Isoc}(X,Y)\rightarrow\mathbf{Isoc}(X^{\prime},Y^{\prime})

admits a simultaneous left and right adjoint
f_{*}
. Moreover, for any
\mathscr{F}\in\mathbf{Isoc}(X,Y)
,
\mathscr{G}\in\mathbf{Isoc}(X^{\prime},Y^{\prime})
the compositions

\displaystyle\mathscr{F}\rightarrow f_{*}f^{*}\mathscr{F}\rightarrow\mathscr{F}

\displaystyle\mathscr{G}\rightarrow f^{*}f_{*}\mathscr{G}\rightarrow\mathscr{G}

are the identity maps of
\mathscr{F}
and
\mathscr{G}
respectively..

Proof.

Let
(X^{\prime\prime},Y^{\prime\prime})\rightarrow(X^{\prime},Y^{\prime})
be a morphism of frames with
Y^{\prime\prime}\rightarrow Y^{\prime}
proper and
X^{\prime\prime}\rightarrow X^{\prime}
a Galois closure of
f
. Let
G
be the Galois group of
X^{\prime\prime}/X
, and
H\leq G
that of
X^{\prime\prime}/X^{\prime}
. The morphism
(f,g)\colon(X^{\prime\prime},Y^{\prime\prime})\rightarrow(X,Y)
of pairs is then one of effective descent for locally free isocrystals: indeed, thanks to
[
Laz22
, Theorem 4.1]
this can be proved word for word the same as the corresponding result
[
Laz22
, Theorem 5.1]
when
Y
is proper.

It therefore follows in the usual manner that
\mathbf{Isoc}(X,Y)
is equivalent to the category of
G
-equivariant objects in
\mathbf{Isoc}(X^{\prime\prime},Y^{\prime\prime})
. Similarly,
\mathbf{Isoc}(X^{\prime},Y^{\prime})
is equivalent to the category of
H
-equivariant objects in
\mathbf{Isoc}(X^{\prime\prime},Y^{\prime\prime})
. I now define
f_{*}
to be the composite

\mathbf{Isoc}(X^{\prime},Y^{\prime})\overset{\cong}{\longrightarrow}H\text{-}\mathbf{Isoc}(X^{\prime\prime},Y^{\prime\prime})\overset{\mathrm{Ind}_{H}^{G}}{\longrightarrow}G\text{-}\mathbf{Isoc}(X^{\prime},Y^{\prime})\overset{\cong}{\longleftarrow}\mathbf{Isoc}(X,Y).

The verification that it satisfies the claimed properties is a straightforward exercise in representation theory.
∎

As I said, this result was proved rather abstractly. With additional hypothesis on
(X,Y,\mathfrak{P})
, however, it is possible to describe
f_{*}
in the expected explicit manner, and generalise from locally free to constructible isocrystals.

3.4.2 Theorem
.

Assume that
\mathfrak{P}
is affine, and that
X
is the complement of a hypersurface in
Y
.

(1)

The functors

{\bf R}\!\left]f\right[_{u\mathrm{dR}*},{\bf R}\!\left]f\right[_{u\mathrm{dR}!}[2d]\colon{\bf D}^{b}(\mathscr{D}_{\left]X^{\prime}\right[_{\mathfrak{P}^{\prime}}})\rightarrow{\bf D}^{b}(\mathscr{D}_{\left]X\right[_{\mathfrak{P}}})

induce isomorphic t-exact functors

{\bf D}^{b}_{\mathrm{cons}}(X^{\prime},Y^{\prime},\mathfrak{P}^{\prime})\rightarrow{\bf D}^{b}_{\mathrm{cons}}(X,Y,\mathfrak{P}),

which send
\mathbf{Isoc}(X^{\prime},Y^{\prime},\mathfrak{P}^{\prime})
into
\mathbf{Isoc}(X,Y,\mathfrak{P})
.

(2)

These functors are both left and right adjoints to

\left]f\right[_{u}^{*}\colon{\bf D}^{b}_{\mathrm{cons}}(X,Y,\mathfrak{P})\rightarrow{\bf D}^{b}_{\mathrm{cons}}(X^{\prime},Y^{\prime},\mathfrak{P}^{\prime}).

(3)

For any objects

\mathscr{F}\in{\bf D}^{b}_{\mathrm{cons}}(X,Y,\mathfrak{P}),\;\;\;\;\mathscr{G}\in{\bf D}^{b}_{\mathrm{cons}}(X^{\prime},Y^{\prime},\mathfrak{P}^{\prime}),

the compositions

\displaystyle\mathscr{F}\rightarrow\mathbf{R}\!\left]f\right[_{u\mathrm{dR}*}\left]f\right[_{u}^{*}\mathscr{F}\cong\mathbf{R}\!\left]f\right[_{u\mathrm{dR}!}\left]f\right[_{u}^{*}\mathscr{F}[2d]\rightarrow\mathscr{F}

\displaystyle\mathscr{G}\rightarrow\left]f\right[_{u}^{*}\mathbf{R}\!\left]f\right[_{u\mathrm{dR}!}\mathscr{G}[2d]\cong\left]f\right[_{u}^{*}\mathbf{R}\!\left]f\right[_{u\mathrm{dR}*}\mathscr{G}\rightarrow\mathscr{G}

are the identity maps.

3.4.3 Remark
.

The additional hypotheses here on
\mathfrak{P}
and
X
are surely unnecessary. However, it seems rather difficult to construct a global comparison morphism between the functors
{\bf R}\!\left]f\right[_{u\mathrm{dR}*}
and
{\bf R}\!\left]f\right[_{u\mathrm{dR}!}[2d]
in full generality, and these hypotheses provide a way of doing this.

The proof of Theorem
3.4.2
will be in several stages.

3.4.4 Lemma
.

It suffices to prove Theorem
3.4.2
under the additional assumptions that
u
is proper, and étale around
X^{\prime}
(i.e.
d=0
).

Proof.

Note that since
\mathfrak{P}
is affine, so is
Y
, and since
X
is the complement of a hypersurface in
Y
, so is
X
. Since
f
is finite étale, it is proved in
[
Stacks
,
Lemma 00U9
]
that
f
is a global complete intersection, for the reader’s convenience I will recall the argument here. Choose a presentation of
X^{\prime}
, that is, a closed immersion
X^{\prime}\rightarrow{\mathbb{A}}^{n-1}_{X}
for some
n
. Let
\mathcal{I}
denote the ideal of
X^{\prime}
inside
{\mathbb{A}}^{n-1}_{X}
. Since
f
is étale, the conormal exact sequence then implies that

\mathcal{I}/\mathcal{I}^{2}\overset{\cong}{\longrightarrow}\Omega^{1}_{{\mathbb{A}}^{n-1}_{X}/X}\otimes_{\mathcal{O}_{{\mathbb{A}}^{n-1}_{X}}}\mathcal{O}_{X^{\prime}},

thus the conormal sheaf of
X^{\prime}
inside
{\mathbb{A}}^{n-1}_{X}
is free. Now choose functions
s_{1},\ldots,s_{n-1}\in\mathcal{I}
lifting a basis of
\mathcal{I}/\mathcal{I}^{2}
. Then Nakayama’s lemma implies that there exists some
t\in 1+\mathcal{I}
such that
\mathcal{I}[1/t]=(s_{1},\ldots,s_{n-1})[1/t]
. Letting
z_{n}
be a new indeterminate, and setting
s_{n}=tz_{n}-1
, it now follows that
X^{\prime}=V(s_{1},\ldots,s_{n})\subset{\mathbb{A}}^{n}_{X}
.

The
s_{i}
can now be successively lifted to provide a proper morphism
\mathfrak{P}^{\prime\prime}\rightarrow\mathfrak{P}
lifting
f
, which étale around
X^{\prime}
. First, homogenizing the
s_{i}
makes
X^{\prime}
into a complete intersection inside
{\mathbb{P}}^{n}_{X}
, given by the zero locus of sections

s_{i}\in\Gamma({\mathbb{P}}^{n}_{X},\mathcal{O}_{{\mathbb{P}}^{n}_{X}}(n_{i}))

for some positive integers
n_{i}
. Chose
s\in\Gamma(Y,\mathcal{O}_{Y})
so that
X=D(s)
. Now clear denominators to ensure that the
s_{i}
extend to sections

s^{\prime}_{i}\in\Gamma({\mathbb{P}}^{n}_{Y},\mathcal{O}_{{\mathbb{P}}^{n}_{Y}}(n_{i})),

and then lift the
s^{\prime}_{i}
to sections

\tilde{s}^{\prime}_{i}\in\Gamma(\widehat{{\mathbb{P}}}^{n}_{\mathfrak{P}},\mathcal{O}_{\widehat{{\mathbb{P}}}^{n}_{\mathfrak{P}}}(n_{i})).

Note that the Jacobian criterion for smoothness implies that the zero locus

V(\tilde{s}^{\prime}_{1},\ldots,\tilde{s}^{\prime}_{n})\subset\widehat{{\mathbb{P}}}^{n}_{\mathfrak{P}}

is étale over
\mathfrak{P}
in a neighbourhood of
X^{\prime}
.

I now let
\mathfrak{P}^{\prime\prime}
denote the maximal closed formal subscheme of
V(\tilde{s}^{\prime}_{1},\ldots\tilde{s}^{\prime}_{n})
which is flat over
\mathfrak{P}
. Thus the locally closed immersion
X^{\prime}\rightarrow V(\tilde{s}^{\prime}_{1},\ldots\tilde{s}^{\prime}_{n})
factors through
\mathfrak{P}^{\prime\prime}
. Let
Y^{\prime\prime}
denote the closure of
X^{\prime}
inside
P^{\prime\prime}
. The upshot of all of this is that there exists morphism of frames

\textstyle{X^{\prime}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{f}
\textstyle{Y^{\prime\prime}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{g^{\prime}}
\textstyle{\mathfrak{P}^{\prime\prime}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{u^{\prime}}
\textstyle{X\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{Y\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{\mathfrak{P}}

such that
u^{\prime}
is proper, and étale around
X^{\prime}
. But now repeated applications of Theorem
3.2.1
imply, in the usual way, that proving the theorem for
(f,g^{\prime},u^{\prime})
is equivalent to proving the theorem
(f,g,u)
.
∎

In the case where
u
is étale, there is an obvious comparison map between
{\bf R}\!\left]f\right[_{u\mathrm{dR}*}
and
{\bf R}\!\left]f\right[_{u\mathrm{dR}!}[2d]
, namely the ‘forget support’ map.

3.4.5 Lemma
.

In the situation of Theorem
3.4.2
, assume that
u
is proper and that
d=0
. Then the natural ‘forget supports’ map

{\bf R}\!\left]f\right[_{u\mathrm{dR}!}\rightarrow{\bf R}\!\left]f\right[_{u\mathrm{dR}*}

is an isomorphism of functors
{\bf D}^{b}(\mathscr{D}_{\left]X^{\prime}\right[_{\mathfrak{P}^{\prime}}})\rightarrow{\bf D}^{b}(\mathscr{D}_{\left]X\right[_{\mathfrak{P}}})
.

Proof.

The natural map
X^{\prime}\rightarrow u^{-1}(X)
is a closed immersion (since it is a proper, locally closed immersion), and since
u
is étale around
X^{\prime}
, there exists an open subscheme
U\subset u^{-1}(X)
containing
X^{\prime}
which is étale over
X
. It follows that
X^{\prime}\rightarrow U
is both étale and a closed immersion, thus
X^{\prime}
is in fact open in
U
. Hence
X^{\prime}
is both open and closed in
u^{-1}(X)
. Hence
\left]X^{\prime}\right[_{\mathfrak{P}^{\prime}}
is both open and closed in
\left]u^{-1}(X)\right[_{\mathfrak{P}^{\prime}}=u^{-1}\left]X\right[_{\mathfrak{P}}
. Since
u
is proper, the ‘forget supports’ map

{\bf R}u_{\mathrm{dR}!}\rightarrow{\bf R}u_{\mathrm{dR}*}

is an isomorphism of functors from
{\bf D}^{b}(\mathscr{D}_{u^{-1}\left]X\right[_{\mathfrak{P}}})
to
{\bf D}^{b}(\mathscr{D}_{\left]X\right[_{\mathfrak{P}}})
. Since
\left]X^{\prime}\right[_{\mathfrak{P}^{\prime}}
is open and closed in
u^{-1}\left]X\right[_{\mathfrak{P}}
, we therefore deduce the ‘forget supports’ map

{\bf R}\!\left]f\right[_{u\mathrm{dR}!}\rightarrow{\bf R}\!\left]f\right[_{u\mathrm{dR}*}

is an isomorphism as required.
∎

Proof of Theorem
3.4.2
(
1
).

We may assume that
u
is proper, and that
d=0
, in which case

{\bf R}\!\left]f\right[_{u\mathrm{dR}!}\rightarrow{\bf R}\!\left]f\right[_{u\mathrm{dR}*}

is an isomorphism. It remains to show that these functors induce t-exact functors

{\bf D}^{b}_{\mathrm{cons}}(X^{\prime},Y^{\prime},\mathfrak{P}^{\prime})\rightarrow{\bf D}^{b}_{\mathrm{cons}}(X,Y,\mathfrak{P}),

or in other words that the functor
{\bf R}\!\left]f\right[_{u\mathrm{dR}*}
sends any

\mathscr{F}\in\mathbf{Isoc}_{\mathrm{cons}}(X^{\prime},Y^{\prime},\mathfrak{P}^{\prime})\subset{\bf D}^{b}_{\mathrm{cons}}(X^{\prime},Y^{\prime},\mathfrak{P}^{\prime})

to an object of

\mathbf{Isoc}_{\mathrm{cons}}(X,Y,\mathfrak{P})\subset{\bf D}^{b}_{\mathrm{cons}}(X,Y,\mathfrak{P}).

This claim is additive over exact sequences in
\mathscr{F}
, moreover, every stratification of
X^{\prime}
admits a refinement which is the pullback via
f
of a stratification of
X
. We may therefore assume that there exists a locally closed subscheme
i\colon Z\rightarrow X
, and a locally free isocrystal
\mathscr{G}
on
Z^{\prime}:=f^{-1}(X)
, such that, writing
i^{\prime}
for the induced immersion
i^{\prime}\colon Z^{\prime}\rightarrow X^{\prime}
,
\mathscr{F}=i^{\prime}_{!}\mathscr{G}
. By Noetherian induction, I can always replace
Z
by an open subscheme, hence I can ensure
Z
shares the hypothesis with
X
that it is the complement of a hypersurface inside its closure in
P
.

Writing
f^{\prime}\colon Z^{\prime}\rightarrow Z
for the induced morphism, transitivity of proper pushforwards implies that

\displaystyle{\bf R}\!\left]f\right[_{u\mathrm{dR}!}\mathscr{F}

\displaystyle={\bf R}\!\left]f\right[_{u\mathrm{dR}!}i^{\prime}_{!}\mathscr{G}

\displaystyle=i_{!}{\bf R}\!\left]f^{\prime}\right[_{u\mathrm{dR}!}\mathscr{G}.

Thus, replacing
X
with
Z
, I can assume that
\mathscr{F}
itself is locally free on
\left]X^{\prime}\right[_{\mathfrak{P}^{\prime}}
. Now, thanks to Proposition
3.4.1
above, there exists a locally free isocrystal
\mathscr{G}
on
(X,Y,\mathfrak{P})
such that
\mathscr{F}
is a direct summand of
\left]f\right[_{u}^{*}\mathscr{G}
. Thus it suffices to prove the claim for
\mathscr{F}=\left]f\right[_{u}^{*}\mathscr{G}
, which, via the projection formula (Lemma
1.4.6
), reduces to the case
\mathscr{F}=\mathcal{O}_{\left]X^{\prime}\right[_{\mathfrak{P}^{\prime}}}
.

To prove the result in this case, note that since
u
is étale in a neighbourhood of
X^{\prime}
, the non-étale locus of
u\colon\mathfrak{P}^{\prime}_{K}\rightarrow\mathfrak{P}_{K}
is a closed analytic subspace of
\mathfrak{P}^{\prime}_{K}
disjoint from
\left]X^{\prime}\right[_{\mathfrak{P}^{\prime}}
. It follows that
\Omega^{\bullet}_{\left]X^{\prime}\right[_{\mathfrak{P}^{\prime}}/\left]X\right[_{\mathfrak{P}}}=\mathcal{O}_{\left]X^{\prime}\right[_{\mathfrak{P}^{\prime}}}
, thus
{\bf R}\!\left]f\right[_{u\mathrm{dR}!}\mathcal{O}_{\left]X^{\prime}\right[_{\mathfrak{P}^{\prime}}}={\bf R}\!\left]f\right[_{u\mathrm{dR}*}\mathcal{O}_{\left]X^{\prime}\right[_{\mathfrak{P}^{\prime}}}={\bf R}\!\left]f\right[_{u*}\mathcal{O}_{\left]X^{\prime}\right[_{\mathfrak{P}^{\prime}}}
, and I will show that
{\bf R}\left]f\right[_{u*}\mathcal{O}_{\left]X^{\prime}\right[_{\mathfrak{P}^{\prime}}}
is a coherent
\mathcal{O}_{\left]X\right[_{\mathfrak{P}}}
-module sitting in degree
0
.

I first show that
{\bf R}\!\left]f\right[_{u*}\mathcal{O}_{\left]X^{\prime}\right[_{\mathfrak{P}^{\prime}}}
has coherent cohomology sheaves. To see this, note that since
u
is proper,
u:u^{-1}\left]Y\right[_{\mathfrak{P}}\rightarrow\left]Y\right[_{\mathfrak{P}}
is also proper, and hence by applying Proposition
1.4.7
to the Cartesian diagram

\textstyle{u^{-1}\left]X\right[_{\mathfrak{P}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{u}
\textstyle{u^{-1}\left]Y\right[_{\mathfrak{P}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{u}
\textstyle{\left]X\right[_{\mathfrak{P}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{\left]Y\right[_{\mathfrak{P}}}

shows that
\mathbf{R}u_{*}\mathcal{O}_{u^{-1}\left]X\right[_{\mathfrak{P}}}
has coherent cohomology sheaves on
\left]X\right[_{\mathfrak{P}}
. Since
\left]X^{\prime}\right[_{\mathfrak{P}}
is open and closed in
u^{-1}\left]X\right[_{\mathfrak{P}}
(as was shown during the proof of Lemma
3.4.5
),
\mathbf{R}\!\left]f\right[_{u*}\mathcal{O}_{\left]X^{\prime}\right[_{\mathfrak{P}^{\prime}}}
is a direct summand of
\mathbf{R}u_{*}\mathcal{O}_{u^{-1}\left]X\right[_{\mathfrak{P}}}
, and thus also has coherent cohomology sheaves.

I next show that the higher cohomology sheaves of
{\bf R}\!\left]f\right[_{u*}\mathcal{O}_{\left]X^{\prime}\right[_{\mathfrak{P}^{\prime}}}
vanish. To see this, consider
\mathbf{R}^{q}u_{*}\mathcal{O}_{u^{-1}\left]Y\right[_{\mathfrak{P}}}
for some
q>0
, which is a coherent sheaf on
\left]Y\right[_{\mathfrak{P}}
. After restricting to
\left]X\right[_{\mathfrak{P}}
, this contains
\mathbf{R}^{q}\!\left]f\right[_{u*}\mathcal{O}_{\left]X^{\prime}\right[_{\mathfrak{P}^{\prime}}}
as a direct summand. Now Proposition
1.3.3
implies that there exists an open neighbourhood
V
of
\left]X\right[_{\mathfrak{P}}
in
\left]Y\right[_{\mathfrak{P}}
, and a decomposition

\left.\left(\mathbf{R}^{q}u_{*}\mathcal{O}_{u^{-1}\left]Y\right[_{\mathfrak{P}}}\right)\right|_{V}\cong\mathscr{F}_{1}\oplus\mathscr{F}_{2}

such that
\mathscr{F}_{1}|_{\left]X\right[_{\mathfrak{P}}}=\mathbf{R}^{q}\!\left]f\right[_{u*}\mathcal{O}_{\left]X^{\prime}\right[_{\mathfrak{P}^{\prime}}}
. Let
\left]X\right[_{\mathfrak{P}}^{\circ}
and
\left]X^{\prime}\right[_{\mathfrak{P}^{\prime}}^{\circ}
denote the interiors of
\left]X\right[_{\mathfrak{P}}
and
\left]X^{\prime}\right[_{\mathfrak{P}^{\prime}}
respectively. Since
u
is proper, and étale in a neighbourhood of
X^{\prime}
, we know that the map

u\colon\left]X^{\prime}\right[^{\circ}_{\mathfrak{P}^{\prime}}\rightarrow\left]X\right[^{\circ}_{\mathfrak{P}}

of adic spaces is étale, and in fact every point
x\in\left]X\right[^{\circ}_{\mathfrak{P}}
has only finitely many preimages under this map. Hence, by Proposition
1.4.7
, the stalk of
\mathbf{R}^{q}\!\left]f\right[_{u*}\mathcal{O}_{\left]X^{\prime}\right[_{\mathfrak{P}^{\prime}}}=\mathbf{R}^{q}\!\left]f\right[_{u!}\mathcal{O}_{\left]X^{\prime}\right[_{\mathfrak{P}^{\prime}}}
at any maximal point of
\left]X\right[^{\circ}_{\mathfrak{P}}
is zero. The support of
\mathscr{F}_{1}
is therefore a closed analytic subspace of
V
, not containing any maximal point of
\left]X\right[^{\circ}_{\mathfrak{P}}
. It is therefore disjoint from
\left]X\right[_{\mathfrak{P}}
, and so
\mathbf{R}^{q}\!\left]f\right[_{u*}\mathcal{O}_{\left]X^{\prime}\right[_{\mathfrak{P}^{\prime}}}=0
.

The last thing left to prove is that the connection on
\left]f\right[_{u*}\mathcal{O}_{\left]X^{\prime}\right[_{\mathfrak{P}^{\prime}}}
is overconvergent, which follows from
[
LS07
, Theorem 4.3.9]
, since any set of local co-ordinates on
(X,Y,\mathfrak{P})
is also a set of local co-ordinates on
(X^{\prime},Y^{\prime},\mathfrak{P}^{\prime})
. Thus neither the derivations, nor the spaces of local sections
\Gamma(V^{\lambda}_{\delta},\mathcal{E})
, occurring in the statement of the Theorem, change on passing from
j^{\dagger}_{X^{\prime}}\mathcal{O}_{\left]Y^{\prime}\right[_{\mathfrak{P}^{\prime}}}
to
\left]g\right[_{u*}j^{\dagger}_{X^{\prime}}\mathcal{O}_{\left]Y^{\prime}\right[_{\mathfrak{P}^{\prime}}}
(at least up to cofinality in
\lambda
).
∎

Proof of Theorem
3.4.2
(
2
) and (
3
).

Again, I can assume that
u
is proper, and that
d=0
. Then
{\bf R}\!\left]f\right[_{u\mathrm{dR}*}
is clearly a right adjoint to
\left]f\right[_{u}^{*}
, since this is already true as a functor

{\bf D}(\mathscr{D}_{\left]X^{\prime}\right[_{\mathfrak{P}^{\prime}}})\rightarrow{\bf D}(\mathscr{D}_{\left]X\right[_{\mathfrak{P}}}).

I therefore need to prove that
{\bf R}\!\left]f\right[_{u\mathrm{dR}*}
is also a left adjoint. To construct the counit

\varepsilon_{\mathscr{F}}\colon{\bf R}\!\left]f\right[_{u\mathrm{dR}*}\left]f\right[_{u}^{*}\mathscr{F}\rightarrow\mathscr{F},

I appeal to Proposition
2.1.6
(note that
{\bf R}\!\left]f\right[_{u\mathrm{dR}*}={\bf R}\!\left]f\right[_{u\mathrm{dR}!}
here) to show that

\mathscr{F}\otimes^{{\bf L}}_{\mathcal{O}_{\left]X\right[_{\mathfrak{P}}}}{\bf R}\!\left]f\right[_{u\mathrm{dR}*}\mathcal{O}_{\left]X^{\prime}\right[_{\mathfrak{P}^{\prime}}}\overset{\cong}{\longrightarrow}{\bf R}\!\left]f\right[_{u\mathrm{dR}*}\left]f\right[_{u}^{*}\mathscr{F}.

Now, by uniqueness of (right) adjoints, I know that
{\bf R}\!\left]f\right[_{u\mathrm{dR}*}
coincides on locally free objects with the abstract adjoint coming from Proposition
3.4.1
. The counit

\varepsilon_{\mathcal{O}_{\left]X\right[_{\mathfrak{P}}}}\colon{\bf R}\!\left]f\right[_{u\mathrm{dR}*}\mathcal{O}_{\left]X^{\prime}\right[_{\mathfrak{P}^{\prime}}}\rightarrow\mathcal{O}_{\left]X\right[_{\mathfrak{P}}}

may therefore be tensored with
\mathscr{F}
to provide the required morphism

\varepsilon_{\mathscr{F}}\colon{\bf R}\!\left]f\right[_{u\mathrm{dR}*}\left]f\right[_{u}^{*}\mathscr{F}\rightarrow\mathscr{F}.

I next construct the unit

\eta_{\mathscr{G}}\colon\mathscr{G}\rightarrow\left]f\right[_{u}^{*}{\bf R}\!\left]f\right[_{u\mathrm{dR}*}\mathscr{G}

in an entirely similar way. Indeed, I first construct a natural morphism

(3.4.1)

\left]f\right[_{u}^{*}{\bf R}\!\left]f\right[_{u\mathrm{dR}*}\mathscr{G}\rightarrow\mathscr{G}\otimes^{{\bf L}}_{\mathcal{O}_{\left]X^{\prime}\right[_{\mathfrak{P}^{\prime}}}}\left]f\right[_{u}^{*}{\bf R}\!\left]f\right[_{u\mathrm{dR}*}\mathcal{O}_{\left]X^{\prime}\right[_{\mathfrak{P}^{\prime}}}

of endofunctors of
{\bf D}^{b}_{\mathrm{cons}}(X^{\prime},Y^{\prime},\mathfrak{P}^{\prime})
. To do this, note that by adjunction, it suffices to construct

{\bf R}\!\left]f\right[_{u\mathrm{dR}*}\mathscr{G}\rightarrow{\bf R}\left]f\right[_{u\mathrm{dR}*}(\mathscr{G}\otimes^{{\bf L}}_{\mathcal{O}_{\left]X^{\prime}\right[_{\mathfrak{P}^{\prime}}}}\left]f\right[_{u}^{*}{\bf R}\left]f\right[_{u\mathrm{dR}*}\mathcal{O}_{\left]X^{\prime}\right[_{\mathfrak{P}^{\prime}}}).

By the projection formula (note that
{\bf R}\left]f\right[_{u\mathrm{dR}*}\mathcal{O}_{\left]X^{\prime}\right[_{\mathfrak{P}^{\prime}}}
is a locally free isocrystal) this amounts to producing a natural morphism

{\bf R}\!\left]f\right[_{u\mathrm{dR}*}\mathscr{G}\rightarrow{\bf R}\!\left]f\right[_{u\mathrm{dR}*}\mathscr{G}\otimes^{{\bf L}}_{\mathcal{O}_{\left]X\right[_{\mathfrak{P}}}}{\bf R}\!\left]f\right[_{u\mathrm{dR}*}\mathcal{O}_{\left]X^{\prime}\right[_{\mathfrak{P}^{\prime}}}.

For this, we just take the natural map
